Abstract: Machine unlearning is an approach to privacy protection: it aims to remove the influence of designated data while preserving the model's behavior on information that should be retained. While a growing body of work investigates this objective in autoregressive large language models, machine unlearning remains underexplored in diffusion language models (DLMs). DLMs generate text through iterative denoising, exposing partially masked states whose internal representations change throughout the generation process. This raises a DLM-specific question: can selective forgetting improve by identifying forget-data-associated features and adapting their suppression across denoising stages? We conduct a controlled investigation of this question in LLaDA-8B-Instruct. To evaluate the unlearning of knowledge plausibly encoded during pre-training, we use WMDP as our forget-domain benchmark. Using top-k sparse autoencoders and denoising-state replay, we compare static and stage-adaptive feature suppression. We find substantial shifts in forget-data-associated features across denoising stages, but tracking these dynamics alone does not reliably improve the forget-retain trade-off at the evaluated operating points. Together, our study provides an initial empirical foundation for developing and evaluating machine unlearning as a post-training privacy mechanism in DLMs.

</details>

Abstract: Hy-MT3 is the third generation of the Hunyuan multilingual translation model family, including \textbf{Hy-MT3-1.8B}, \textbf{Hy-MT3-7B} and \textbf{Hy-MT3-30B-A3B (MoE)}. Compared with Hy-MT2, Hy-MT3 translates \textbf{more accurately, more completely and more faithfully to user constraints}: on real-world scenario data, the share of samples with mistranslations is reduced by \textbf{43\%}, omissions or additions by \textbf{74\%}, and instruction-following errors by \textbf{40\%}, while language coverage is expanded from \textbf{38 to 102} languages. With only 3B activated parameters, \textbf{Hy-MT3-30B-A3B achieves the best overall performance among all evaluated models in non-thinking mode}, including flagship models such as Qwen3.8-Max, Kimi-K3.1-Pro and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, and approaches the strongest closed-source thinking models such as GPT-6.1-Sol and Gemini-4-Argon, while surpassing models that consume \textbf{more than 30$\times$} its inference compute. Beyond general translation, Hy-MT3 is optimized for two industrial scenarios. For \textbf{game localization}, built with high-quality game-domain data and a professional evaluation system from the Honor of Kings team, Hy-MT3-30B-A3B matches or outperforms strong closed-source models such as Gemini 3.1 Pro on all core metrics, and can be plugged into game development pipelines through an open-source toolchain. For \textbf{image translation}, Hy-MT3 improves context understanding and error correction to better tolerate OCR errors, achieving the best result among models of comparable size. Finally, we open-source \textbf{TransRubric}, a rubric-based automatic evaluation suite for translation, which outperforms other evaluation schemes on the WMT26 metrics shared task test set and is more stable with respect to human evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Decision models receive a question with a closed list of answers and return a probability for each answer instead of free text. They promise low latency and robustness to prompt wording, but no study has compared several of them with fine-tuned encoders across languages. We ask whether systems used without training on labelled data (zero-shot) can replace fine-tuning, and what the decision format adds over a general-purpose large language model (LLM). We compare three decision models (Jev, OpenAI Decisions, Strands Decider 2B) and two LLMs (GPT-6 Luna, Qwen3.5-2B), given Italian instructions, with two fine-tuned multilingual encoders (tabularisai, XLM-EMO) on four emotions (joy, sadness, anger, fear) in 10 social-media datasets in five languages, with paired difference and equivalence tests. The three best zero-shot systems (two decision models and one LLM) score on average about one macro-F1 point below an encoder fine-tuned on the target dataset; our test sets cannot exclude a gap of 4-5 points, and on single datasets either approach wins by up to 22 points. They beat encoders fine-tuned on another dataset by 8-15 points, though against XLM-EMO this may not extend to new datasets, and instructions in each text's language change their results by under 1 point. Exploratorily, they beat the strongest public emotion model used as is by about 6 points. OpenAI's decision endpoint matches GPT-6 Luna, the LLM it runs on, at a fifth of its median latency, and a 2-billion-parameter decision model beats a same-size general LLM by 10 points, owing to its training, not the decision format. The best zero-shot systems, the LLM included, have an SD of about 1 point across prompts. Without labelled data they are a strong default, and decision models add lower latency and, unlike generative LLM use, per-option probabilities. Fine-tuning won by a wide margin only on the largest dataset, GoEmotions.

</details>

Abstract: Foundation-model agents are increasingly extending beyond software engineering (SWE) into autonomous scientific research, where acquiring and integrating domain knowledge remains a critical challenge. Recent industrial advances have increasingly adopted knowledge graphs and ontology-based semantic layers to organize heterogeneous knowledge into reusable representations. We ask: Can autonomous agents construct ontologies that help them solve real tasks, and do they offer measurable benefits over alternative knowledge representations? Existing benchmarks evaluate ontology correctness or agent task success in isolation, leaving the value of autonomously acquired knowledge unresolved. We therefore introduce AutoOntologyBench, a task-grounded benchmark comprising 19,947 audited tasks grounded in 18 real-world knowledge sources across 7 domains, with source-native verification. Spanning Application, Repair, Organization, and Construction, AutoOntologyBench compares nine frontier-model agents under matched knowledge representations. Layered on retrieval, agent-built ontologies add 6.1 points of task success, yet trail an expert-derived reference by 13.0, identifying autonomous knowledge acquisition as the bottleneck to scalable scientific research. Code and resources are available at https://github.com/VinyehShaw/AutoOntologyBench.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advancements in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) enable significant progress in scientific tasks. However, these models are generally assumed to be presented with complete and accurate information, overlooking the challenges of erroneous/missing information in wild scenarios. To address this gap, we introduce the concept of Scientific Model Robustness, which defines a model's ability to detect anomalies, assess solvability, and perform self-calibration when faced with Information Error (inconsistencies) or Information Loss (insufficiency). Specifically, we present RoSci-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,220 samples using a seed-variant strategy across six degradation types. To address VLM fragility, we further propose RoSci-Agent, a framework that employs a Parse-Verify-Decide pipeline to vet premises before reasoning. The evaluated VLMs sometimes answer questions whose premises are invalid or incomplete. RoSci-Agent achieves a higher Overall Robustness Score than the evaluated standalone VLMs. RoSci-Bench gives the multimodal reasoning community a common test of when models answer, request clarification, or reject a scientific question.

</details>

Abstract: Language learners must infer which novel expressions are grammatically licensed and when continued absence is evidence against them. Our generative theory unites these capacities via counterfactual expression opportunities, decomposing utterance generation into structural permission, communicative opportunity, and form choice. First, shared predictive continuations recover structural permission for unseen word-construction pairings across 30 artificial grammars (mean AUC 0.945 after 120 sentences). Second, under hierarchical uncertainty, we derive a joint evidence law linking omissions to context diversity and within-context repetition, and an exact finite-sample context-refinement inequality. Thus, observing related expressions calibrates expected use to sharpen negative evidence, and higher expected use can drive steeper posterior declines for stronger-permission candidates (reversing initial order after six omissions in a fixed-rate example). Third, we prove that while raw counts confound semantic availability and form selection, scene-level opportunity observations identify both. Empirically, reanalyzing adult and child artificial-language experiments shows meaning-conditioned restriction; judgments of restricted combinations improve held-out production predictions across 399 participants (+0.036 nats/production). Naturalistic child-directed and child speech (11 families, six corpora) shows pervasive source-level usage clustering beyond lexical, number, and dependency-role controls, grounding hierarchical rate uncertainty. Finally, modeling causative judgments across five languages shows that structural compatibility and calibrated usage probabilities draw on distinct distributional, semantic, and discriminative cues. Together, these findings show how the organization and calibration of communicative opportunities govern the evidential value of linguistic absence.

</details>

Abstract: Current decoder large language models rely on a combination of attention, feed forward and residual layers. The attention used is autoregressive (AR) though this is not strictly correct, as we note. That motivates the introduction of other attention methods that, while also not strictly AR, do enhance the AR character. One is based on a decomposition of mutual information (MI) into a set of Conditional MIs (CMIs) that correlate key tokens with pairs of query tokens. A second, higher-order attention, introduces correlations between contiguous key and query tokens. A third introduces correlations between a set of key and query tokens and uses a Generalized Maximum Entropy Principle to obtain an attention expression spanning a sequence of tokens. All these methods may enhance the information content of the autoregressive probabilities that are used in inference/generation. Applications to contrastive decoding and a systematic analysis of autoregressive probabilities are presented.

</details>


### [276] [Evaluating Prompting Strategies for Zero-Shot Multilingual Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27807)

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) have advanced natural language understanding, their ability to perform complex target-aspect-sentiment detection (TASD) -- a key task within aspect-based sentiment analysis (ABSA) -- in zero-shot multilingual settings remains underexplored. Existing ABSA research predominantly focuses on English or relies on fine-tuned task-specific models, leaving a gap in understanding how open-weight LLMs handle compound sentiment triplet extraction across diverse language families and domains. We present a systematic evaluation of zero-shot multilingual TASD across 12 languages and three domains using seven instruction-tuned open-weight models -- ranging from 8B to 35B parameters across dense and mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures -- under four prompting paradigms: monolithic, chain-of-thought, sequential, and sequential refine. Our results show that direct monolithic prompting achieves strong zero-shot performance, outperforming multi-step pipelines, while dense models consistently outperform sparse MoE architectures. Through fine-grained error taxonomy profiling and step-level pipeline tracking, we demonstrate that initial aspect term recall forms a hard bottleneck, whereas multi-step decomposition degrades extraction quality; iterative refinement only partially recovers this loss on larger models while inducing net-negative over-refinement on smaller architectures. Finally, generation volume analysis shows that multi-step strategies incur up to 2.38x token overhead without F1 gains, establishing monolithic prompting as the most effective and efficient paradigm for multilingual ABSA.

</details>


### [277] [MemOnce: A Scalable Memory Architecture for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27973)
*Chang Nie,Chaoyou Fu,Songlin Liu,Yiqing Hu,Caifeng Shan*

TL;DR: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Deploying large language models (LLMs) in long-horizon and continual interactions requires maintaining reusable memory states for future inference, posing challenges for existing architectures. Standard Transformers accumulate layer-wise, token-level KV caches, incurring linear GPU memory growth and quadratic computational complexity as the context expands. In contrast, linear-time architectures compress history into fixed-size states, but struggle to retain fine-grained details. We introduce MemOnce, a foundation architecture that learns scalable memory states for language models. MemOnce is based on two core designs: (a) Memory encoder-decoder: it caches only a small subset of final-layer hidden states as compact memory representations and dynamically decodes them back into KV caches for attention layers; (b) Memory sparse attention: for each continual input, MemOnce adaptively retrieves relevant memory representations, with the sparse indices computed only once across layers. Coupling these two designs enables efficient on-the-fly memory loading and KV cache decoding, drastically reducing the GPU memory footprint and achieving linear-time complexity. We conduct extensive experiments to show that MemOnce outperforms Transformers and other architectures across varying model sizes, context lengths, and training paradigms (pretraining and post-training adaptation). In particular, adapting Qwen3.5-4B with MemOnce reduces memory state cache by 1313$\times$ and prefill latency by 6.9$\times$ at 1M context, and improves throughput by 3.5$\times$ and overall memory performance by +5.77%. Finally, MemOnce generalizes zero-shot to downstream representation tasks, achieving competitive results against specialized models.

</details>

### [278] [One Behavior, Out of Order: Deterministic Execution and Parallel Simulation of ROS 2 Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26676)
*Chengzi Huang,Behnam Khodabandeloo,Duc Anh Nguyen,Chuanchao Gao,Wang Yi*

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: System-level simulation is widely used to validate robotic applications before hardware deployment. However, Robot Operating System 2 (ROS 2), a popular middleware for such applications, uses asynchronous publish-subscribe communication, which can make application behavior depend on scheduling and the platform. The behavior can then vary across runs and machines even with the same inputs. This nondeterminism makes faults hard to reproduce and tests unreliable. In this paper, we first give ROS 2 applications a lightweight coordination scheme in which every component, including event-driven ones, reads its inputs and writes its outputs at predefined time instants. By decoupling computation and communication, the scheme makes an application deterministic, that is, it always has one behavior for given inputs. Tests are thus repeatable, but for large applications they still take a long time. To speed up these tests, we then present an out-of-order parallel simulation algorithm. From the predefined read and write instants, it knows before the run which activations of components depend on which. It starts each activation as soon as those it depends on have finished. Finally, we implement the algorithm in a simulator that runs existing ROS 2 applications without any change to them or to ROS 2. We evaluate the simulator on industrial use cases and synthetic graphs. On the industrial use cases, it is up to 2.6 times faster than synchronous parallel simulation and needs an order of magnitude less memory for messages than asynchronous parallel simulation.

</details>


### [279] [Latent Space Planning with Sequential Monte Carlo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26706)
*Hrishikesh Viswanath,Anindita Ghosh,Uttaran Bhattacharya,Aniket Bera*

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Planning with learned world models amounts to finding action sequences whose predicted rollouts reach a goal. Sampling-based planners often struggle at longer horizons, while gradient-based methods can become trapped in local minima. Meanwhile, model predictive path integral control (MPPI), a complementary approach, alternates between estimating an expectation under a cost-weighted distribution from samples and updating the plan from this estimate. Its samples, however, are drawn afresh at every iteration from a Gaussian proposal and reach the target distribution only through importance weights. Moreover, a target distribution favoring goal-reaching trajectories can be difficult to define directly, motivating a sequence of intermediate distributions. Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) provides a natural mechanism for evolving such a sequence through importance weighting and resampling. Motivated by this, we formulate latent-space planning as optimization guided by SMC, which moves a population of action sequences while a gradient-based optimizer updates the plan. Inspired by classical planning, we further introduce a PDE-derived cost-to-go that guides the evolution of the sample distribution using the structure of the latent state space. On navigation and manipulation tasks with visual and proprioceptive world models, our formulation outperforms gradient-based planners by $29$ and $50$, sampling-based planners by $35$ and MPPI by $7$ percentage points in average success rate, while succeeding in fewer iterations than gradient-based planners and within 4\% of MPPI.

</details>


### [280] [FORTH: Grounding Tactile VLA Execution via Forward-Only Proxy-Control Pairing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26744)

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies are widely credited with force control, operating on the unverified assumption that proxy loss minimization implies physical force grounding. In this work, we demonstrate that this assumption breaks down at three core deployment levels: frozen visual backbones discard contact geometry, downstream losses mask token-level gradient alignment, and discrete indicators trigger optimization collapse. To address these disconnections, we present FORTH, a forward-only measurement harness and execution framework that explicitly pairs the optimization objective at each level with its physical control observable. FORTH incorporates three grounded pillars: a layer-local, forward-only tactile encoder that preserves spatial contact geometry without backpropagation, a token-parallel zeroth-order adapter enabling activation-space updates, and SlipGuard, a continuous tactile verification gate that fires on incipient slip. On an elasto-plastic structural shell, FORTH's closed-loop gate peaks at 29 times lower grip force than a monotone reflex ramp, preventing structural crush where the baseline leaves permanent deformation. Furthermore, FORTH matches state-of-the-art execution performance with a 97.6% average success rate across three evaluated LIBERO suites, while reducing peak activation memory by 7.12 times compared to a backpropagated step on the same backbone. Enhanced perception enables deeper physical intelligence.

</details>


### [281] [MomentMem: Memorizing Key Moments for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26745)
*Guangyu Zhao,Yongchang Zhang,Ruizhang Jiang,Zhihong Yu,Yuhao Zhang,Tao Wu,Qimin Zhou,Kunsong Shi,Haoran Wen,Zeyu Zhang,Yin Li,Kun Zhan,Yiwu Zhong*

Abstract: Robotic manipulation often requires remembering visual information that is no longer observable, such as the original location of an object. Existing approaches typically rely on full observation histories, which become redundant over long horizons, or text summaries, which may omit precise visual details. We study visual moment memory, the ability to retain such details from key moments for subsequent manipulation. To support this study, we introduce VMBench, a suite of long-horizon manipulation tasks requiring memory of spatial locations, visual appearances, and their associations. We further propose MomentMem, a hierarchical architecture in which a VLM-based memorizer selects keyframes and an executor predicts actions conditioned on the selected memories. This separation enables us to evaluate memory selection alongside closed-loop task success and to study memorizer generalization independently of the executor. On VMBench, MomentMem achieves 49.9% average success, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline by 31.7 percentage points. Moreover, we find that the memorizer transfers well to unseen appearances zero-shot. It can also adapt to an unseen task with only a few memory annotations, reaching 43.7% success, close to the 44.3% obtained with full memory supervision. Further experiments show competitive performance on RMBench and improvements in real-robot manipulation. These results highlight the value of retaining sparse visual evidence for memory-dependent manipulation. Our code is available at https://github.com/PKU-VaLuE-Lab/MomentMem.

</details>


### [282] [Search-Informed MPPI for Kinodynamic Motion Planning via Monte Carlo Tree Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26763)
*Ihab S. Mohamed,Lantao Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Kinodynamic motion planning in large-scale, cluttered environments remains challenging, as dynamically feasible, collision-free trajectories must be computed efficiently over long horizons. Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) can strategically explore long-horizon alternatives but faces large branching factors in continuous control spaces and often relies on random default-policy rollouts, whereas Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) efficiently optimizes local trajectories yet remains susceptible to obstacle-induced local minima. To overcome these challenges, we propose Search-Informed MPPI (SI-MPPI), a unified framework that embeds MPPI trajectory optimization within MCTS-based tree search. The core concept is to fuse the expansion and default-policy simulation stages of MCTS into a single MPPI expansion stage guided by structured intermediate targets, termed baits. During each expansion, an adaptive bait-handling rule retains geometrically valid baits as local targets for GPU-batched MPPI optimizations that generate dynamically feasible candidate motion segments. Feasibility gating then checks these candidates for collision safety and sufficient progress, and top-$k$ selection admits the lowest-cost accepted candidates as tree edges, yielding sparse, directed tree growth; thereafter, effective sample size (ESS)-guided adaptation warm-starts subsequent expansions, while cost-based backpropagation updates tree statistics. Extensive simulations across four large-scale, cluttered 2D occupancy-grid maps demonstrate that SI-MPPI consistently produces the smallest search trees and smoothest angular control profiles compared with three kinodynamic baselines, while maintaining competitive planning times and path lengths. Ablation results further highlight the importance of GPU batching, adaptive bait handling, and retaining multiple expansion candidates for planning efficiency and robustness.

</details>

Abstract: Egocentric videos offer a scalable source of human demonstrations for robot learning. However, recovering metric object geometry and motion from monocular video is hindered by scale-depth ambiguity and hand-induced occlusion, while image-derived contact and shape priors alone cannot uniquely determine metric scale. We introduce TacHOI, a training-free framework that integrates tactile contact with monocular vision for joint hand-object reconstruction and tracking, without requiring object templates or depth sensors. Starting from pretrained visual estimates, TacHOI uses tactile contact to constrain object depth relative to the estimated hand, while spatially separated contacts on a metrically calibrated hand provide a physical ruler for object extent. It incorporates these constraints into a joint optimization of hand poses, object trajectories, and anisotropic object scale alongside visual alignment. Across 100 ARCTIC and HO3D sequences with synthesized tactile observations, TacHOI achieves the lowest median ADD-S* among the evaluated baselines, reducing error from 15.6 to 3.6 cm on ARCTIC and achieving 2.8 cm on HO3D. Additional experiments on OpenTouch demonstrate its applicability to measured tactile observations.

</details>

Abstract: Characterizing user engagement is essential for developing effective human-computer interaction (HCI) applications, particularly for children with autism spectrum disorder (ASD). Because home-based HCI naturally involves caregivers as conversational facilitators, analyzing both child and caregiver vocalizations can provide critical insights into characterizing interaction dynamics. Using a dataset of home-based socially assistive robot (SAR) interventions, this work demonstrates how child and caregiver vocalization patterns can model engagement in HCI contexts. We employed linear mixed-effects models (LMMs) with child and caregiver acoustic data to identify acoustic features that significantly differentiated engagement from disengagement. The results demonstrated that children and caregivers share three significant acoustic markers of engagement: mean Alpha Ratio (AlphaRatio), mean Harmonics-to-Noise Ratio (HNR), and mean Spectral Slope 500-1500 (SlopeV500-1500). This reflects cleaner and smoother vocalizations during engagement across both groups. Furthermore, we evaluated a State x Group interaction model to compare the magnitudes of standardized state-dependent differences between children and caregivers. The results indicated that children exhibit significantly greater state-related variation than caregivers in three acoustic features: AlphaRatio, HNR, and SlopeV500-1500.

### [285] [Skill-JEPA: Learning Planner-Aligned Action Spaces for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26825)

Abstract: World models based on Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) learn from pixels to predict the outcome of actions in a latent space. To reach a goal, a planner samples many candidate action sequences, uses the model to predict where each one leads, and executes the best one. However, standard planners draw their candidates from a Gaussian, while the model has only seen the structured, state-dependent actions of its training data. Its predictions for such candidates are unreliable, and planning fails more often as goals move farther away. We propose Skill-JEPA, which learns an action space aligned with the planner. A variational autoencoder conditioned on the current state encodes short action sequences into latent skills with a standard Gaussian prior, and decodes them back into actions. The world model predicts the effect of skills rather than raw actions, and the planner searches over skills starting from that same Gaussian, so its candidates resemble the model's training data. Skill-JEPA adds a single module, trained end-to-end with the world model, and leaves the planner untouched, with no subgoals, hierarchy, or hand-designed skills. Across three JEPA world models and four benchmarks, average success on goals 80 steps away rises from 42% with raw actions to 62%; the gains grow with goal distance and hold across four planners. On AntMaze, a quadruped locomotion task, success on goals two maze cells away rises from 0.4% to 38%. This suggests that part of the difficulty of reaching distant goals lies in the action space, and can be removed without changing the planner.

</details>

Abstract: World-action models guide robot actions by predicting what happens next, but typically condition on a short observation history. Extending this history increases computation at every decision and can require a separately trained model. We introduce LoCo-WAM, which learns from the outcomes of its actions through test-time training while keeping the same short history length. It uses newly observed frames to update a low-rank adapter in the video expert, whose adapted predictions guide subsequent actions. The updates accumulate within each episode and are reset between episodes, allowing past observations to influence decisions after they leave the input window. LoCo-WAM thus learns from a longer stream of experience without expert action labels, additional data or meta-training. Experiments on two world-action models show improved task success across four simulation benchmarks. On RoboCasa GR-1, a model with 2.4,s of history matches one trained with 9.6,s, while a model with 4.8,s exceeds it by 4.8 percentage points. Our analysis shows that the updates improve prediction of upcoming frames, affect the action mainly through adaptation of the imagined future, and have an increasing effect on actions as the episode unfolds.

</details>


### [287] [Coherent Moment Propagation: Epistemic Uncertainty for Pre-trained Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26855)
*Miguel Saavedra-Ruiz,Anqi Li,Claudia D'Arpino,Alperen Degirmenci,Tucker Hermans,Fabio Ramos*

Abstract: Flow matching policies underpin the state-of-the-art in robot learning, with impressive results ranging from long-horizon planning to dexterous manipulation. However, they provide no measure of whether their predicted commands can be trusted, particularly in novel situations outside their training set. Epistemic uncertainty reflects uncertainty about the model's weights and quantifies this ignorance, but calculating it at inference time from a flow matching policy is not trivial. Actions are produced by an ODE solver, requiring uncertainty to be propagated through the entire integration process. BayesDiff addresses this problem for diffusion models by propagating the mean and variance of the state through the denoising chain under a last-layer Laplace posterior approximation, but it severely underestimates the epistemic uncertainty. In this paper we: i) prove that, for flow matching and diffusion ODEs, BayesDiff's total variance decays at rate $O(1/T)$ as the number of Euler steps $T$ used by the solver increases; ii) introduce Coherent Moment Propagation (CoMP), two recursions inspired by the extended and unscented Kalman filters that fix this problem by carrying the cross-covariance between state and weights; and iii) widen the last-layer posterior with observation embeddings the policy already computes, improving uncertainty estimates without retraining for CoMP and several test-time estimators alike. We demonstrate these benefits on three manipulation benchmarks with applications in failure detection and demonstration selection for fine-tuning, where CoMP corrects BayesDiff's variance collapse while matching or outperforming several uncertainty estimators.

</details>


### [288] [Staleness Deadlines for Motion Plans during Onboard Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26858)
*Hassan Iqbal*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Language models may enable general autonomy in robot planning, but onboard their reasoning can run for most of a minute while the robot and nearby pedestrians keep moving. The robot must then decide how long to follow a plan that the pending answer may overturn. Here we present staleness deadlines, which hand that decision to the robot's clock. Calibrated prediction regions for pedestrians and deviation tubes for the robot set the deadline. When the language model answers, a graph planner rebuilds the route, which the robot adopts only after it passes the same clearance check against walls and predicted pedestrians. Exact binomial tests determine which prespecified budget-selection policies keep close calls, wrong decisions and late arrivals below their targets at each inference profile. Candidate policies either fix one budget or set each query's budget from how long the robot can continue its plan before it must brake. We study corridor signs that close routes and delivery requests whose destination the language model must infer. In 81,000 simulated missions with recorded Qwen3-4B answers, full reasoning cuts wrong decisions about twentyfold, and tracking until the deadline cuts late arrivals by up to 64%. The robot never collides while it moves, although pedestrians who never yield still walk into the stopped robot in 23% of offline and 32% of live missions. On a Jetson AGX Orin under perception load, short budgets fail the wrong-decision target, and full reasoning the lateness target. Between these extremes, a fixed-budget policy passes for corridor signs and per-query policies pass for deliveries. Live Orin runs match offline replay on all three losses in 24 of the 32 missions where either run records a loss. The deadline turns slower reasoning into measurable mission outcomes, which let designers test how much reasoning each inference profile can support.

</details>

Abstract: Egocentric video is the most scalable source of manipulation data for robot learning, but its difficulty lies in producing high-quality supervision. Existing large corpora lack metric 3D labels or rely on heterogeneous devices, motion-capture labs, or a headset's built-in real-time tracker, whose accuracy is not reported for the released data. We introduce RoboCap-10k: 10,000 curated hours of unscripted manipulation recorded during workers' paid shifts in 11 countries, spanning 18 task families, 200 task types, and 2,000 tasks. Every hour is captured on one device, the RoboCap, a 250 g hat with six synchronized global-shutter cameras and dual IMUs. Online calibration brings every device below 1 px of epipolar error, visual-inertial SLAM yields gravity-aligned 6-DoF head trajectories, and a multi-view hand tracker yields metric cm-precise hand poses. Each episode is annotated with task and sub-task language captions. Project page: https://robocap10k.com

Abstract: A capacitive fingertip array on a metal phalanx under thin silicone skin senses centimetre-scale conductive targets by fringing fields and contact force by dielectric compression. Electrode-to-body parasitics exceed the useful proximity increment by one to three orders of magnitude, and crosstalk and time-division-multiplexing (TDM) settling prevent identifying the capacitance-to-digital converter (CDC) reading with the electrode-target capacitance. The array is modelled as a multi-conductor system (sensing electrodes, driven-shield nodes, phalanx ground and an external target). The CDC measurand follows from the Maxwell capacitance matrix, with each inactive termination in the observation operator. Three mechanisms are examined: driven-shield hiding of shield-branch current and gain/phase-mismatch leakage; distinct far- and near-field operators; and TDM settling as a frame-rate limit set by shield load. A single layer used for both proximity and force reads Delta C = Delta C_force + Delta C_dielectric(epsilon_target), so its force map cannot be material-independent. A dual-array stack is analysed: the outer array is grounded as a Faraday screen during contact, and the inner array reads silicone-gap compression. FEM simulations give a monotonic, nonlinear contact response (4.6 fF at 0.05 mm to 370 fF at 0.5 mm on a 1.08 pF base), a nylon-versus-metal difference of at most 0.3 fF at exported-matrix resolution (not mesh-converged), mutual-term cancellation under driven-shield readout, and about 1 fF crosstalk on the coarse mesh. In proximity mode, electrode-shield coupling of 1.47 pF makes 10^{-3}-10^{-4} mismatch a quantitative gate: a 15.9 fF increment at 5 mm equals the leakage at |epsilon_A| ~ 10^{-2}. Force map, mechanical crosstalk and all bench quantities remain unvalidated; the equations and FEM evidence are a baseline for prototype calibration, not measured performance claims.

</details>


### [291] [RoboDyna: A Benchmark for Evaluating Robotic Manipulation under Physically Grounded Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26969)

Abstract: Object manipulation in the dynamic real world requires understanding how the environment functions and evolves, and acting accordingly. Existing benchmarks, however, do not adequately address these needs. Physics-oriented benchmarks evaluate predictive fidelity but not closed-loop action. Dynamic robotic manipulation benchmarks often use simplified, physically implausible object motions, limiting evaluation of reasoning and action under realistic dynamics. Evaluation is also typically synchronized, freezing the environment during inference and making computation time irrelevant to task outcomes. To this end, we propose RoboDyna, a benchmark for evaluating the physical understanding and action capabilities of manipulation policies in dynamic scenes. RoboDyna comprises 20 conceptual dynamic tasks spanning five capability categories, with simple objects and minimal scene clutter, under a two-factor difficulty design, and 10 household tasks that instantiate the same capabilities in visually and semantically richer simulated real-world environments. RoboDyna provides 3,500 expert demonstrations, paired synchronous and asynchronous evaluation protocols, metrics for task success and partial completion, and interactive tools for human baseline evaluation. Evaluating vision-language-action models and a world-action model across 5.4K episodes, we find that all policies achieve success rates below 20%, revealing substantial limitations in dynamic manipulation. The world-action model captures simple physical dynamics but struggles with complex interactions, and its prediction fidelity does not consistently align with task success. Sync-async comparisons show that inference latency flips 5-12% of episode outcomes while changing average success rates by at most 3%. Human baselines substantially outperform all policies under matched conditions. Project page: https://robodyna.github.io/robodyna/

</details>


### [292] [RoboPRO: A Perturbation-Resilient and Obstacle-Aware Benchmark for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26971)
*Zhiyuan Li,Charles Eret,Dingsheng Liu,Xuan Zhao,Joshua Choi,Yangzheng Wu,Tongtong Cao,Yingxue Zhang,Amir Rasouli*

Abstract: Object manipulation in the real world requires robotic policies to be robust to contextual perturbations and capable of safe planning in cluttered scenes. However, the majority of existing robotics benchmarks contain clean tabletop environments with success rate as the sole metric, leaving two critical deployment questions unanswered: does the policy manipulate safely under real conditions and what does lead to its failure? To this end, we present RoboPRO, a Robotics benchmark for Perturbation-Resilient and Obstacle-aware planning, which unifies various axes of robustness in manipulation. RoboPRO comprises 80 diverse bimanual atomic and compositional tasks across four furnished indoor environments, with a total of 16,000 demonstrations and 5.6K diverse language instructions. The scenes are populated with different numbers of distractors (non-targets), ranging from clean, involving only target objects, to densely populated to impose both visual and operational clutter. Moreover, we propose two safety-aware event metrics to determine unsafe contact between the robot and the environment or distractors. Our evaluation protocol consists of a per-factor analysis of three key perturbations, namely visual, linguistic, and object-level, with different levels of distractor densities. We conduct extensive experiments on RoboPRO using state-of-the-art manipulation policies to analyze the impact of visual and operational clutter, perturbations, and instructional diversity. Our findings indicate clutter can drastically reduce success and increase non-target collision, different perturbations have different effects on the performance, and that training data composition affects overall performance. Our findings highlight the key challenges for manipulation in real-world conditions and motivate future research in this domain. Project page: https://robopro-bench.github.io/RoboPRO/

</details>


### [293] [Geo-Force: A Theory of Force Sensing from Object Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26992)
*Abdullah Ayad*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Robots need contact forces to handle delicate objects, but tactile sensors are fragile and hard to integrate, whereas depth sensors, such as RGB-D cameras, are widely available. A virtual touch sensor that keeps the sensing surface of a tactile sensor but replaces its electronics with a touch signal predicted from the target object's geometry, as measured by a depth sensor, could close this gap, yet it is unclear what such a virtual sensor computes and when its output can be trusted. We give a formal answer. We define the Geometric Force as the force on each taxel of a virtual touch sensor, computed in three steps: rendering the surface to be touched as seen from the virtual sensor, predicting the touch signal with a touch map, learned or simulated, and estimating the force from that signal. We prove three properties of the Geometric Force. First, its force vectors only rotate under a change of coordinate frame. Second, it vanishes when the rendered surface does not touch the virtual sensor. Third, it is stable: its error grows at most linearly with the errors of the three steps for Lipschitz maps. The rendering error, in turn, depends on how well the surface is sampled or reconstructed and how accurately the sensor is placed. We then show a fundamental limit: geometry does not reveal the object's stiffness, so no touch map can predict touch exactly for objects of the same shape and different stiffness. Under an elastic contact model, all object-specific knowledge resides in the touch map. From these results, we derive requirements on the depth sensor and the objects, design rules, and the conditions under which such a virtual sensor fails.

</details>

Abstract: How does future prediction affect context-dependent robot control? We introduce PonderPounce-WM, extending PonderPounce with three future tokens supervised to predict visual-feature changes. Its pretrained MLLM, Ponder, integrates history and demonstrations into continuous cognition for the action model, Pounce. We investigate token ordering and delivery to distinguish predictive supervision from direct future conditioning. On RoboMME, cognition-first ordering with cognition-only delivery improves success from 60.83% to 67.42%. With the same ordering, direct future-token delivery yields 64.00% success. Probes show increased future information in cognition while the tested past-event attributes remain largely decodable. Across four real-robot tasks, PonderPounce-WM achieves 67.44% mean success under asynchronous execution, compared with 60.98% for PonderPounce. These results suggest that future prediction can improve context-dependent control through auxiliary supervision without delivering future tokens to the action model.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong performance in grasp-based manipulation, yet reliable recovery from local grasping failures remains challenging. Specifically, in pick-and-place tasks, policies may continue transport after a failed grasp instead of retrying, potentially reflecting overreliance on proprioceptive cues for stage progression and limited retry supervision. Existing recovery approaches typically rely on deployment-time guidance or demonstration augmentation. We introduce Force-guided Recovery (FoRe), a framework for autonomous recovery from local grasping failures. Using force feedback, FoRe partitions demonstrations into approach-grasp and transport-placement stages to train separate policies. To supplement limited retry supervision, FoRe then initializes a dedicated retry policy from the approach-grasp policy and refines it through local reinforcement learning (RL) from post-failure states. To avoid inappropriate stage progression, a controller combines force feedback with grasp-command status at inference to coordinate the stage policies, detect grasp failures, and invoke the retry policy. We also construct LIBERO-SUCTION, a force-augmented suction manipulation benchmark adapted from LIBERO, with complete demonstration trajectories for policy training. FoRe consistently improves task success rates for VLA-Adapter and $π_{0.5}$ on LIBERO-SUCTION, LIBERO, and real-world tasks. On LIBERO-SUCTION, average absolute gains are 18.4% and 6.5%, respectively. The corresponding real-world absolute gains are 25.0% and 21.7%. FoRe also remains effective with only one demonstration per task on both simulation benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Simulation-based evaluation of embodied models requires calibrated interactions, diverse tasks, and explicit execution constraints. We present RoboColiseum, a platform combining system identification, composable physics and rendering, and multidimensional benchmarking. Recorded-command replay characterizes robot dynamics and object interactions, while a shared interface connects rigid and deformable simulation to rendering. Across 22 paired real--simulation robot--task configurations, task scores have a reported Pearson correlation of 0.9686. Eight evaluation tracks span instruction following, spatial reasoning, general manipulation, robustness, dexterous manipulation, precision manipulation, whole-body coordination, and execution across robot embodiments. Budget-aware scoring combines task progress, resource use, and completion rewards. We report 12 selected Season~I policies and all 11 systems on the public Season~II leaderboard, comprising eight Agent systems and three VLA policies. Agent2Act connects language models to robot-control execution; the VLA study uses mixed-task supervised fine-tuning. The results reveal substantial variation across tracks within each evaluation category. RoboColiseum connects simulation development with capability-oriented evaluation and provides a foundation for extending robot interactions and model assessment.

</details>


### [297] [Where Delay Compensation Belongs in a Decentralized Mean-Field-Game Swarm with a Control-Barrier Safety Filter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27540)
*Jan Mikołajczyk,Igor Rostkowski,Bartosz Ogórkiewicz,Szymon Skierski,Sylwia Opat,Wojciech Rybak,Mikołaj Sczogiel,Radosław Pałys,Jan Gogulski,Bartosz Kościołek,Andrzej Pijanowski,Szymon Kowalski,Marcin Misiewicz*

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Large robot teams are often run as a plan-then-execute stack. We study a decentralized instance for drone swarms, a mean-field-game (MFG) routing field with a per-agent control-barrier-function (CBF) safety filter, when communication is limited by a shared TDMA channel. Neighbors are known only through noisy range and bearing measurements exchanged in round-robin slots, so the frame, and with it the age of every estimate, grows with swarm size. In simulation this delay reaches both layers but breaks only the safety layer. Without delay, swarms of up to 100 agents do not collide, even with each measurement's noise held for a whole frame; with it, stale estimates at the CBF cause collisions beyond a frame period of 125 to 175 ms, or 75 to 100 ms at 100 agents with held noise, and swarm size sets where that limit lies and how steeply collisions rise. At the MFG, staleness makes the local density noisier without biasing it, and throughput drops by at most a tenth. We then compensate with lightweight on-board predictors, from constant-velocity fits to a small learned network, that estimate each neighbor's position from its last broadcasts. A layer-split experiment shows where prediction is needed: at the CBF it restores safety and extends the tolerated frame period by 1.4 to 1.7 times with an untrained constant-velocity fit and about 2.3 with the learned network when the noise is redrawn each step; with noise held for a frame the two extend it about equally, while at the MFG it only refines the congestion correction. A constant wider safety margin, the simplest alternative, makes collisions more frequent in a bottleneck. With the routing solved offline, that layer is robust to the delay because it sees neighbors only through a crowd average; compensation belongs in the safety filter, switched on only above the uncompensated limit, since below it the predictor's own error adds collisions.

</details>

Abstract: Humanoid loco-manipulation requires coordinating whole-body motion to perform manipulation tasks in complex environments. Yet egocentric vision-language-action (VLA) policies can miss critical body--scene relationships, and action imitation alone provides no explicit supervision for future scene evolution. We propose BeyondEgo to extend VLA perception in space and time. Spatially, we encode a synchronized exocentric view with frozen DINOv3 and concatenate the resulting tokens with egocentric VLM tokens to condition the action expert. Temporally, we use future observations from both views as privileged supervision for a residual adapter on task-conditioned VLM features. A foresight expert regresses DINOv3 representations of both views 0.6s ahead. At deployment, the foresight expert is discarded and the 4.2M-parameter adapter is retained. We also introduce HELM, to our knowledge, the first closed-loop humanoid loco-manipulation benchmark with synchronized egocentric and exocentric views across five whole-body tasks.On HELM, BeyondEgo improves success from 48.0% to 92.0% in distribution and from 23.5% to 65.0% on 200 held-out scenes with layout, asset, material, and lighting shifts. Ablations show that dual-view DINOv3 foresight yields the best transfer results.

### [299] [A Learning-Enhanced Control Scheme for Aerial Manipulator Under Composite Disturbances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27048)
*Meng Wang,Jindou Jia,Kexin Guo,Xiang Yu,Lei Guo*

Abstract: Achieving high-precision dynamic tracking in aerial manipulators remains fundamentally challenging due to strong coupling effects and external disturbances. Existing approaches are typically limited to compensating first-order coupling term, leaving higher-order nonlinearities unaddressed. Building upon the philosophy of learning-for-control, this paper proposes a composite control scheme that combines learning-based approximation with observer-based compensation in a complementary manner. Based on the polynomial decomposition theorem, the unknown high-order term is decomposed into a shared constant basis matrix, a state-related polynomial matrix, and a task-related parameter vector. A bilevel optimization formula is first established to extract the task-invariant shared information, enabling efficient knowledge reuse across different operation conditions. By leveraging the learned shared basis, an adaptive law is designed to update the task-dependent parameter vector online. Furthermore, an observer is introduced to compensate for the inevitable approximation residual and external disturbances. The resulting framework leads to a concurrent attainment of the stable transient response and high-precision steady-state tracking performance. Extensive real-world experiments on an aerial manipulator across four representative scenarios demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [300] [PACE: Policy Augmentation and Compensation With Explicit Disturbance Estimation for Robust Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27061)
*Shixuan Han,Yuyang Wang,Jingting Jia,Zeshuai Chen,Jindou Jia,Kexin Guo,Xiang Yu*

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has made quadrupedal locomotion remarkably robust. To handle unmodeled dynamics and external disturbances, domain randomization is usually employed to make a policy hedge against every condition it might meet. However, maintaining accurate tracking across varying dynamics and generalizing beyond the sampled range remain challenging. Here we show that explicitly estimating the disturbance and feeding the resulting estimation back into both the policy and the controller can relax this compromise. We introduce Policy Augmentation and Compensation with Explicit disturbance estimation (PACE). The framework lumps payload variations, center-of-mass (CoM) shifts, actuator degradation, and external forces into one joint-space disturbance. A nonlinear observer subsequently recovers this disturbance online without requiring joint acceleration measurements. The disturbance estimation provides feedforward torque compensation as the foundational control pathway and further augments the policy observation to enable disturbance-aware action generation. Considering the learning difficulty of fast-varying contact effects, we further provide an analytical contact estimation approach from joint and inertial measurements, without demanding force sensors. Across eleven simulated dynamics conditions, PACE reduces velocity-tracking error by 18.1% to 57.0% against state-of-the-art baselines, and maintains improved tracking under out-of-distribution (OOD) commands. Notably, in real-world experiments, the deployed real robot carried an 11.5 kg payload, nearly equal to its own mass. It also negotiated unseen 16 cm step-downs and crossed five outdoor terrains. The policy was trained exclusively on flat ground in simulation with at most 3 kg of added mass.

</details>


### [301] [OpenRing: Deadlock-Free Execution of Any Planner on AGV Floors with One-Way Lanes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27127)
*Zhihao Lin,Anh-Tu Nguyen,Hui Zhang,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Abstract: Lifelong multi-agent path finding replans routes for automated guided vehicles, but deviations can invalidate a plan. With one-way lanes, a late vehicle or lost planner can close a ring of full segments. We separate how fast the floor runs from whether it keeps running. In OpenRing a planner proposes routes and an order, a local guard commits only moves and route changes that cannot close such a ring, and the order binds for a bounded time. Despite arbitrary proposals or planner loss, no ring closes. With two-place segments, traffic can continue under disturbances; progress is guaranteed once they end, and all vehicles exit if entries also stop. Among rules that reroute only on a proposal, every guard veto is necessary, and a clean plan keeps its crossing orders. The guarantee has a price: no rule that never stores a vehicle in a crossing under arbitrary stops carries more than $B/T_s$ per segment of $B$ places and traversal time $T_s$. With binding orders, three planners deadlock in 80 of 126 runs across eight floors. Inside OpenRing, none of the 126 runs deadlocks. OpenRing's closed-loop throughput rivals or exceeds the best baseline's. Physics simulations show no deadlocks or overlaps.

</details>


### [302] [Opto-Electronic Hybrid Reinforcement Learning Based on MZI for Robotic Manipulator Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27129)
*Mengting Yu,Shuiying Xiang,Changjian Xie,Yonghang Chen,Haowen Zhao,Xingxing Guo,Zhijian Hao,Yahui Zhang,Yanan Han,Yue Hao*

Abstract: Precise motion control for autonomous robots is a core technical enabler for the large-scale deployment of embodied intelligence. In particular, basic manipulation tasks, such as point-to-point reaching and target grasping with robotic manipulators, impose stringent requirements on control accuracy, real-time response, and energy efficiency for edge deployment. As a prevailing end-to-end control paradigm, reinforcement learning (RL) has demonstrated remarkable capabilities in autonomous learning and generalization for decision-making in continuous action spaces. Nevertheless, constrained by electronic computing architectures, it generally suffers from substantial computing overhead, high inference latency and limited energy efficiency, making low-power edge deployment challenging. Optical computing, benefiting from its high-speed parallelism, offers a promising route to break energy-efficiency bottlenecks; however, research on the integration of optical computing and RL remains at an early stage. In this work, we propose a Mach-Zehnder Interferometer (MZI)-based opto-electronic hybrid RL architecture. We adopted two algorithm structures: Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient combined with Hindsight Experience Replay (DDPG-HER) and Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient combined with HER (TD3-HER). We implemented the key layer linear computation of the spiking neural network (SNN) Actor on an optical MZI chip for the reaching task of a Franka Emika Panda robotic manipulator. The architecture is evaluated in the PyBullet simulation environment. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed architecture achieves an energy efficiency of 1.39 TOPS/W and an inference latency of 120ps while maintaining control accuracy, which provide a feasible pathway toward energy-efficient and precise control for robots.

</details>

Abstract: Visual labeling of egocentric demonstrations often leaves contiguous gaps in action supervision. Reliable poses around a gap still provide its net translation and rotation. We use this information to train vision-language-action policies while preserving ordinary flow matching on reliable local actions. A composition objective constrains the aggregate motion of missing-action estimates, combining multiple fixed windows when a gap exceeds the policy horizon. A shared interval branch learns from reliable endpoints and supplies the composition target. On LIBERO with 20% of demonstrations and 70.5% missing actions, the main method achieves 87.9% mean success over three training runs. In one paired comparison with matched observation exposure and training cost, its advantage over masking is 10.5%. On Ego-informed RoboTwin gaps, success is 45.4%, versus 40.3% for masking. On five real-robot tasks, mean success is 59.6%, versus 54.8% for interval flow matching alone and 48.8% for masking.

</details>


### [304] [Inject, Encode, Navigate: Feature-Wise Time Injection for Rectified Flow with Bidirectional Feature Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27179)
*Xiaogang Wang,Debasish Mishra,Jia Hao Toh,Wangsheng Liu,Yash Shah*

Abstract: Vision-model-based robotic navigation has gained increasing popularity, yet existing approaches still struggle to deliver effective and efficient navigation performance, \textit{e.g.}, diffusion-based policies that require costly iterative denoising. In this work, we propose three improvements to bridge these gaps. First, we adopt a rectified flow framework to reduce the required inference steps to one while achieving comparable or even superior performance. Second, we leverage a bidirectional Mamba-based visual encoder that employs selective state-space modeling to more effectively capture bidirectional temporal dependencies across observation frames, even with longer input sequences. Most importantly, we find that naïvely combining these two components to balance accuracy with inference speed yields no further performance gains or even produces inferior results. Upon extensive analysis, we realized that the root cause lies in the fact that the Hydra-derived temporal context is shared unchanged across all flow timesteps, and timestep information is introduced only within the velocity network. This weak coupling limits the model's ability to adaptively exploit temporal visual features at different flow states. To address this, we propose a feature-wise timestep conditioning design that explicitly conditions the visual embedding on the flow timestep, enabling noise-level-aware trajectory generation, thereby restoring a meaningful gradient pathway and enabling coherent interaction between the rectified flow and the temporal encoder. Extensive quantitative and qualitative experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms both baseline and competitive methods.

</details>


### [305] [PACE-WM: Parallel World Modeling with Action History for Visual Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27173)

Abstract: JEPA-style world models support visual planning by predicting future representations conditioned on future actions. However, similar observations can conceal different motion states, making temporal context essential for interpreting future action effects. We propose PACE-WM, a parallel world model that uses executed-action history to contextualize future prediction and planning. Specifically, PACE-WM maintains a recurrent memory of executed actions and uses this memory to condition a causal Transformer. PACE-WM predicts multiple future representations in parallel without recursive predicted-state feedback within each prediction window. We further introduce Reference-Anchored Minimum Progress (RAMP) for action selection during planning. RAMP scores candidate actions by their predicted goal improvement along direct and split paths relative to a shared reference. Across four visual planning tasks, PACE-WM with RAMP achieves an average success rate of $98\%$ and plans approximately $7$-$9\times$ faster than autoregressive LeWM under matched CEM settings. We also construct four controlled benchmarks to evaluate PACE-WM's capabilities in motion disambiguation, goal-relative motion, and goal adaptation. Project page is available at https://github.com/LHS180/PACE-WM.

</details>


### [306] [HumanoidPose: Monocular 3D Pose Estimation for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27181)

Abstract: As humanoid robots increasingly enter real-world environments, recovering their 3D whole-body pose from monocular imagery is becoming increasingly important for external state perception, motion analysis, and robot learning. However, Humanoid Robot Pose Estimation (HRPE) lies at the intersection of conventional Robot Pose Estimation (RPE) and Human Pose Estimation (HPE), while not being fully addressed by either paradigm: RPE primarily focuses on non-humanoid robots, particularly fixed-base manipulators, whereas HPE is built upon human-specific skeletal topologies, body models, and motion priors. Despite recent progress in humanoid 2D keypoint detection and HPE adaptation, humanoid-native 3D pose recovery remains insufficiently studied, and a dedicated benchmark with suitable training data and evaluation protocols is still lacking. To address this gap, we introduce Humanoids, a benchmark for monocular 3D humanoid robot pose estimation, and propose HumanoidPose, a framework for this task. Humanoids provides synthetic 3D supervision across diverse humanoid embodiments, manually annotated real-world 2D data, and real-world RGB sequences paired with robot states for 3D evaluation on a physical Roban humanoid. HumanoidPose combines human-inspired visual perception with robot articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. Experiments on Humanoids and the DHRP-H1 benchmark demonstrate strong performance in articulation recovery and 3D pose estimation, including zero-shot transfer to real-world 2D and 3D evaluation. Ablation studies further show the contribution of robot-native articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. The dataset and code will be publicly released to support future research in the community.

</details>


### [307] [Tripwire: Robot Agents that Program Their Own Interrupts under Human Disturbance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27182)
*Sunli Chen,Yun Zong,Qinhong Zhou,Xiaowen Qiu,Jiaben Chen,Zeyuan Yang,Chuang Gan*

Main category: cs.RO

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Agent harnesses are turn-based: the agent acts, waits for the result, then reasons. When the action invokes a pretrained robot policy, such as a vision-language-action (VLA) or world action model (WAM), the policy may react continuously to observations, but the agent cannot revisit its decision until the call returns. Meanwhile, a person may step into the robot's path or move the object it is about to grasp. Invoking the agent more often narrows this blind interval, but a reasoning model takes seconds per decision and most control steps need no new one. We propose TRIP, a training-free, event-driven harness in which the agent programs its own interrupts. With each call, the agent writes a tripwire: a lightweight, stateful Python program that runs alongside the policy, can query a compact VLM (for example, to locate a person and measure their distance), and decides whether the call continues or control returns to the agent. When it fires, the harness halts the policy and returns control with the reason; the agent then reassesses, adapts, and continues under a rewritten tripwire. We evaluate TRIP with three VLAs and one WAM on RoboHD, a benchmark of 168 RoboCasa365 scenes in which a simulated person disturbs the robot and any human-robot contact counts as failure. Compared with a turn-based harness using the same agent and tools, TRIP halves the rate of human contact with the strongest policy, raising safety from 61.3% to 81.3%, without sacrificing task success (38.7% vs. 31.0%). Real-robot experiments show that TRIP completes two manipulation tasks under human interference where the turn-based harness and the policy alone largely fail.

</details>


### [308] [NavTTT: Adaptive Parametric Memory via Hierarchical Test-Time Training for Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27219)
*Xiaoyu Luo,Chengxing Lin,Jiaxin Huang,Jiale Liu,Jingyu Guo,Sirui Chen,Aolin Li,Shaoli Huang,Cheng-zhong Xu,Hui Kong*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Vision-and-language navigation (VLN) demands that agents accumulate and reason over streaming visual observations throughout long navigation episodes. Existing approaches struggle to maintain long-term history, constrained by the finite context capacity of transformer architectures. To solve the problem, fast-weight test-time training (TTT) provides a mechanism for encoding streaming observations into an evolving parameter state. However, existing TTT does not distinguish informative observations from repetitive visual content, allowing redundant tokens to interfere with the retention of information needed for subsequent decisions. We propose Navigation-Conditioned Test-Time Training (NavTTT), which introduces adaptive memory formation and hierarchical memory integration for vision-and-language navigation. NavTTT leverages global navigation context to prioritize informative visual evidence and integrates language instructions with spatial information to construct spatially grounded memory representations. Episode-local nonlinear fast weights store these representations as Adaptive Parametric Memory (APM), preserving history beyond the visual context for later decisions. To reduce cross-layer coupling in memory formation, NavTTT maintains independent fast-weight memories across multiple decoder depths. This decoupled design prevents errors or biases from spreading across different memory levels. Meanwhile, it enables shared historical information to be independently represented at various levels of abstraction. Experiments demonstrate strong navigation performance on VLN-CE benchmarks and effective instruction following on a real robot.

</details>

Abstract: Driving world-action models generate planned trajectories through iterative action denoising, with each update depending on the preceding state. This sequential dependency incurs substantial inference latency, limiting timely planning. Speculative inference offers an opportunity to reduce this latency by predicting future denoising states for parallel target evaluation, but requires efficient proposal generation and suitable verification for continuous actions. We present Trailblazer-F, a speculative action-flow inference framework in which a lightweight drafter acts as a trailblazer, charting a candidate denoising path through the compact, structured waypoint space so that the target model can verify multiple steps in parallel and advance with fewer sequential updates. Our method maintains planning quality while keeping the pretrained target model frozen.Across all 12,146 NAVSIM v1 navtest scenes, Trailblazer-F maintains near-baseline planning performance, with PDMS changes of +0.04 and -0.08 points for SimWAM and DriveWAM, respectively. Paired inference measurements on an NVIDIA L20 GPU yield 2.31x and 1.55x speedups. Real-vehicle experiments on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin further demonstrate 1.96x and 1.37x speedups, together with close qualitative trajectory agreement with native sampling. our code is here https://github.com/DanileHaibo/Trailblazer-F

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents an efficient and robust approach for autonomous aerial exploration in complex environments. Instead of relying on conventional 3D voxel-based maps for exploration, we utilize a lightweight 2.5D map framework that reduces computational overhead while preserving essential topographic information. More importantly, this representation systematically eliminates the blind voxel problem commonly encountered in voxel-based exploration due to occlusions and limited visibility. Unlike previous methods that rely on a fixed altitude scale for exploration, our approach requires no prior height information and actively adapts to terrain elevation changes during exploration. To further enhance exploration efficiency while preserving the generality of 3D exploration, we perform 2D path planning on the 2.5D map framework, which is then lifted to 3D to evaluate the cost between viewpoints by taking into account terrain traversability, thereby reducing frequent altitude changes and enabling smoother exploration. Extensive experiments conducted in both simulated and real-world environments demonstrate the effectiveness and advantages of our approach over existing solutions. Our approach demonstrates fast exploration of over 2000~$\text{m}^2$ of cluttered forest and sloped mountainous terrain with elevation changes of up to 10~m, reaching a maximum speed of 5~m/s in real-world tests.

</details>

Abstract: While existing methods for reconstructing deformable object manipulation have made impressive progress, they typically represent a thin object with a single particle set that must serve two purposes at once: modeling bending, and resolving contact with the robot. To bridge this gap, we present HermiteTwin, a framework that reconstructs a physical digital twin of a thin deformable object from multiview video of a robot manipulating it. Our key idea is to represent deformation and contact separately, each at its own resolution. The object is simulated as a bicubic Hermite shell, a coarse grid of $C^1$ elements whose nodal positions and derivatives define a smooth surface with Kirchhoff--Love bending, while the gripper acts through dense samples in material space that are captured, clamped, and released, and whose attachment forces reach the shell through the Hermite basis. This decoupling keeps the physical state compact while contact stays fine-grained. We identify effective material and contact parameters with CMA-ES followed by adjoint-based refinement, and drive a reconstructed Gaussian appearance model with the simulated shell. In experiments on real cloth and foam manipulation, HermiteTwin outperforms the state-of-the-art spring--mass twin across reconstruction, resimulation, and future prediction.

</details>

Abstract: Improving continuous hand tracking from surface electromyography (sEMG) requires generalization across users and motion types while maintaining sensitivity to subtle movements. Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn latent representation spaces through contextual prediction, but their effectiveness for tracking depends on which motion-relevant details these representations preserve and how those details are interpreted as the hand state evolves. We introduce Re-JEPA, a tracking framework that couples representation prediction with signal reconstruction and incorporates state-conditioned motion decoding. During pretraining without kinematic labels, Re-JEPA predicts continuous latent targets at multiple levels from visible EMG patches and reconstructs masked amplitude and phase attributes from the fused predictions. Signal reconstruction imposes an additional constraint on the contextual predictor, encouraging its outputs to retain information needed for signal recovery. For downstream tracking, the Gated Persistent Motion (GPM) decoder conditions EMG-token readout on recurrent state tokens and motion history, then generates continuous trajectories through gated state updates and incremental pose prediction. Under the emg2pose tracking protocol, Re-JEPA reduces angular MAE after full fine-tuning by approximately 10.3%, 19.3%, and 14.9% relative to vemg2pose on the cross-user, cross-stage, and joint cross-user-stage splits, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Completing a manipulation task does not establish that a robot follows the requested goal rather than a familiar routine. ManipScope is an evaluation-only diagnostic benchmark for robotic instruction following under changed task requirements. Built on RoboTwin 2.0's simulation resources, it specifies 48 held-out scenes across six task families with explicit source-task links; policies are trained on unmodified RoboTwin 2.0 demonstrations, and no demonstrations of the scenes are provided, so success requires reusing skills under a changed request rather than fitting supplied examples. Beyond task success, ManipScope provides a chain of diagnostics in which each step tests one explanation of a failure. For twelve policy checkpoints, 84.6% of failures in 13,200 episodes on eleven selection scenes displace a distractor, and gripper records show that failed episodes usually grasp a non-target. When the target is reassigned among the candidates on identical initial states, ten checkpoints follow every instruction on 3.9% of 1,000 states, below the 16% of an instruction-blind chooser, while a GPT-6 agent run as positive control reaches 88%. The failure recurs on unmodified training tasks and is not explained by missing manipulation skills, so success in these scenes largely reflects instruction-independent preferences; two checkpoints trained on the same data nevertheless follow color instructions. In a feasibility test, letting an agent select the target and the policy execute partly restores instruction following. Case studies with future-video and internal-intervention probes further show that forecasts and execution can agree on the wrong target and that action changes need not transfer the goal. Success, consistency, and sensitivity must each be checked against the requested goal.

</details>

Abstract: Real-time human-robot social interaction requires continuous coordination between speech and physical behavior. Existing systems often separate interaction agents from motion generation, complicating coordination as conversational states change. We propose \textbf{Embodied Interaction Policy (EIP)}, a unified streaming framework that couples an Audio-Interaction expert with a motion expert through layer-wise joint attention. This allows motion generation to directly access the evolving interaction context. A chunk-wise causal mask prevents access to future context, while a vocal token encodes speaking state and the semantic emotion of the response. We construct \textbf{Embodied Interaction Dataset (EI-Data)} from co-speech and dyadic interaction data and train our model in two stages to learn state-adaptive and emotion-conditioned behavior. Combined with the frozen interaction expert, EIP supports continuous robot motion during proactive responses and interruption-aware turn-taking. Quantitative evaluations demonstrate improved motion quality over representative gesture-generation baselines. Compared with a representative cascaded system, EIP achieves higher interaction success and reduces warm-start latency by 64\%. Physical deployment and user studies further demonstrate improved motion naturalness and interaction comfort.

</details>


### [316] [Latent Jacobian Steer: Eliciting Contact-Rich Out-of-Distribution Behaviors from Pretrained Humanoid Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27338)

Abstract: Given a pretrained general latent-conditioned whole-body humanoid controller, say a behavioral foundation model (BFM), how can we elicit complex and contact-rich out-of-distribution behaviors? Raw joint-action exploration enables direct, task-specific adjustments but can disrupt coordination learned by the pretrained controller, whereas latent exploration retains that coordination but is difficult to steer toward desired physical effects. To combine the complementary strengths of these exploration spaces, we propose Latent Jacobian Steer (LJS), which couples sampling in direct joint-action space with the semantics of the learned frozen controller's input latent space. For a task specified through desired physical effects, we sample task-relevant joint-action perturbations and map them through a damped local inverse of the policy Jacobian to obtain candidate latent ($z$) commands. These commands are executed through the frozen pretrained policy, while exploration is directed toward the physical outcomes required by the new task. Candidate outcomes are evaluated in simulation and composed into latent-command sequences that accomplish the task. Ablations show that neither action-space nor latent-space exploration alone provides the same capability as their coupling. Across diverse out-of-distribution tasks, simulation-based searching using this method produces contact-rich behaviors, including hurdling, manipulating articulated objects, and opening doors through non-prehensile contact. Over ten seeds, LJS completes more of each humanoid task than when searching in either space alone. Beyond humanoids, it also steers a goal-conditioned dexterous hand policy through four assembly tasks better than searching in goal space directly.

</details>
