Abstract: Action-conditioned video world models imagine future observations under candidate robot actions, providing a promising visual tool for robot policy evaluation and interaction simulation. However, existing video world models often optimize global reconstruction errors instead of emphasizing interaction-relevant regions. As a result, they may preserve overall scene appearance but overlook local interaction details, leading to physically implausible outcomes such as hallucinated contact effects and inconsistent robot-object interactions. We introduce MoReWorld, a unified motion-region-guided framework that uses a shared motion region, the part of the environment that moves in response to the robot's actions, to focus dynamics learning and video generation on interaction-relevant regions. The same motion region guides local motion prediction, region-aware supervision, and motion-gated future-feature conditioning. At inference time, the motion region is predicted as a motion probability map from visual history and robot actions, without requiring future observations or ground-truth motion labels. On WorldSimProbe, MoReWorld achieves the highest overall simulator-faithfulness score, with the strongest performance on local consistency, global consistency, and interaction dynamics. These results indicate that motion-region guidance helps the model preserve scene-level consistency while more faithfully predicting action-conditioned interaction dynamics.

</details>


### [318] [NormFlowSDE: Normalizing Flows for Amortized Bayesian State Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27362)
*Yi Zhang,Lars Mikelsons*

Abstract: Bayesian state estimators, such as Kalman filters, estimate states from noisy observations based on dynamical models. However, obtaining accurate models can be challenging when the system involves effects that are difficult to model faithfully, such as friction, impacts, and deformation in contact-rich manipulation. Uncertainty in system parameters, inputs, and disturbances, together with simplified noise models, can further introduce simulation-to-reality mismatch and degrade estimation accuracy. These limitations motivate learning a distribution over state trajectories conditioned on observations and controls without explicitly specifying the underlying physical models. We introduce NormFlowSDE, an amortized Bayesian state estimator that learns a distribution over state trajectories by combining neural stochastic differential equations with conditional normalizing flows. Its key design choice is to utilize the capability of normalizing flows to learn stochastic behavior of variables, while leveraging the continuous-time dynamics of SDEs to model the evolution of states over time. Once trained, the model draws state paths for new measurement sequences without per-sequence optimization. In addition, we connect NormFlowSDE to stochastic continuous-time dynamics by proving weak convergence of a mean-centered, drift-corrected inference scheme to an Itô diffusion under stated assumptions. Finally, we benchmark NormFlowSDE against direct estimators and learned particle filters based on normalizing flows on partially observed dynamical systems, including simulated systems and real-world robot datasets. The results show that NormFlowSDE attains the lowest estimation error with calibrated uncertainty intervals on both robot datasets and filters four times faster than the fastest particle filter on every dataset.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion models have recently attracted increasing attention in motion planning due to their ability to model high-dimensional and multi-modal trajectory distributions. However, existing diffusion-based planners still struggle to reliably satisfy geometric feasibility in unseen cluttered environments with complex obstacle layouts. To address this challenge, we propose GeoSDF-Diff, a geometry-aware diffusion planner that incorporates multi-scale SDF conditioning into trajectory denoising. The key idea is to provide the denoiser with both route-level geometric context and fine local clearance evidence, enabling generated trajectories to better adapt to unseen complex obstacle layouts. Specifically, a Geometric Encoder extracts multi-scale SDF features and injects them into a DiT-X denoiser through cross-attention. We further introduce a trajectory-level training objective that combines SDF clearance, violation emphasis, smoothness, and path-length regularization, so that local infeasibilities are penalized during training rather than corrected only at inference time. Experiments on held-out cluttered environments show that, without post-hoc trajectory repair or sampling-time guidance, GeoSDF-Diff achieves a 65.2% average success rate across diverse test scenarios, outperforming strong diffusion baselines by 25.4% to 28.5%.

</details>

Abstract: Simulated LiDAR sensors play a crucial role in validating autonomous systems, yet achieving signal-level realism remains challenging due to simplified sensor models and limited configurability in common simulation environments. This paper presents a lightweight, platform-agnostic method to assess one observable aspect of LiDAR simulation fidelity: the range-dependent point density on annotated objects. The method uses only 3D bounding boxes and point clouds and does not require semantic labels, detailed maps, simulator internals, or perception models. To quantify agreement between real and simulated range-dependent point distributions, we formulate the Density Similarity Score (DSS), a bounded similarity representation derived from the Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) over discretized distance bins. The method is evaluated against a real-world LiDAR using three CARLA configurations of varying complexity. Results show that naive parameter matching based on sensor datasheets leads to systematic point-density deviations, particularly in the near field, while a custom configuration using the sensor's non-uniform vertical beam angles achieves the highest similarity in the evaluated scenario (DSS: 0.559--0.709). The proposed approach is intended as an early-stage, signal-level consistency check for identifying gross mismatches in simulated LiDAR configurations. It should be interpreted as a complementary validation step rather than a complete measure of simulation quality or downstream perception performance.

</details>

Abstract: Parametric body models expose incompatible pose and shape controls, tying humanoid policies to particular input formats. AnyBodyCodec learns a shared representation through native conversion, then reuses it for new body models and control with its core frozen. Encoders map MHR, SMPL-X, and SOMA to aligned slots; native decoders recover target controls. The 1.090M-parameter converter covers nine routes and reaches 16.801 mm input-reference MPJPE across six cross routes: the 2.887M Transformer bank reaches 16.390 mm and its parameter-matched version 18.866 mm. Three measured routes take 7.43-7.67 ms on an RTX 4090. Adding ANNY shows that comparable conversion accuracy can accompany different action consistency under one frozen controller. Under a matched PPO budget, aligned slots support 60.78% full-action success across three formats. Separate 160-hour training reaches 66.10% source-weighted validation success, and the controller is deployed qualitatively on a physical G1 through three visual front ends. Together, these results establish reuse through both native geometric fidelity and downstream policy compatibility.

Abstract: Vision-Language Navigation(VLN) requires an embodied agent to follow language instructions through unseen 3D environments, where the target spaces often lie beyond the current observation. Navigation world models address this challenge by predicting future views either as pixels or latent representations, providing valuable look-ahead information for action decision. Both paradigms define the future primarily through what the agent may observe, raising a fundamental question: is predicting future views the ultimate form to represent the future for VLN? Cognitive studies show that the human hippocampus prospectively represents both future goals and intervening locations along planned routes. Inspired by this predictive-map view, we organize the navigation-relevant future around goal-directed spatial states and route structure. We instantiate this perspective with two complementary abstractions: a short-horizon waypoint that specifies the next local state to reach, and a mid-horizon corridor that captures the spatial support of the upcoming route. Based on this design, we propose FS-Nav, a future-state world modeling framework that predicts waypoint-corridor states from spatial memory and language instructions, and communicates them to a VLM navigator through top-down and first-person interfaces. Experiments on standard VLN benchmarks demonstrate that FS-Nav achieves state-of-the-art performance. This highlights navigation-relevant future-state modeling as a promising direction for representing the future in VLN.

</details>

Abstract: Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. During training, ground-truth depth identifies the lowest-error reference within a group of physically evaluated camera settings. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. At deployment, it scores observed captures using frozen-model features and camera, motion, and illumination context, without ground truth or reference images. A contextual Gaussian-process controller uses recent scores to select subsequent exposure and gain settings under motion and photometric constraints. We evaluate offline camera selection with relative and metric Depth Anything v2 and demonstrate online control with the affine-invariant variant on a mobile robot. Across nine equally weighted scene-illumination conditions, ADEPT reduces mean scale-and-shift-aligned $\mathrm{Abs}_{\mathrm{rel}}$ by 14.6% relative to built-in auto-exposure and by 10.0% relative to the best-performing evaluated prior controller.

</details>

Abstract: Pretrained visual generation models have shown great promise in robotic manipulation. However, a representative line of work still treats future visual prediction as the central link between visual generative pretraining and action learning, retaining it as a training objective even when future scenes are no longer generated at inference time. We argue that the instruction-following, spatial understanding, and visual imagination capabilities acquired through image generation pretraining can support control without future visual prediction: image generation models themselves can be directly adapted into powerful action learners. Building on this perspective, we propose GenPA, which fine-tunes an image generation backbone with a lightweight 19M-parameter action regression adapter and reuses its native generative objective for perception co-training. The native image-editing interface accommodates different visual targets without task-specific perception heads, enabling comparisons of current-scene perception, future prediction, and their combinations within a common policy architecture. At deployment, GenPA requires only a single backbone forward pass and action regression, without visual generation or iterative action denoising. Without additional large-scale robot-data pretraining, GenPA demonstrates strong control performance and robustness to perturbations across simulation benchmarks (99.10% success on LIBERO and 83.79% on LIBERO-Plus) and real-world manipulation tasks. Under compiled inference, GenPA takes 20.9 ms per action chunk on a single H100 GPU, achieving 1.7-3.4x speedups over strong VLA and WAM baselines. Our latency analysis further highlights architectural advantages of visual generation backbones for efficient robot control. Ablations across visual tasks and their combinations identify current-depth supervision as a simple, effective training recipe.

</details>

Abstract: Simulation is central to intelligent-vehicle validation, but visual realism or output agreement between a real scene and its synthetic counterpart does not prove that a perception system used the same evidence in both domains. This paper addresses that mechanism-parity gap for paired real-synthetic autonomous-driving perception. It unifies Decisive-Feature Fidelity, which compares decisive evidence maps in a frozen system under test (SUT), with Concept-Decisive Feature Fidelity, which projects decisive evidence into semantic scene concepts for diagnosis. The framework is a validation diagnostic rather than a standalone safety certificate: it asks whether output-consistent simulation also preserves the mechanism by which the perception model reaches that output. The empirical evidence comprises ten retrained diffusion ControlNet generators evaluated on a deterministic exact-geometry KITTI-Virtual KITTI 2 subset. Generated counterparts use a 1248-by-376-pixel padded inference canvas and are cropped back to the exact KITTI dimensions before evaluation. The final benchmark reports target-image, output-consistency, and mechanism metrics recomputed from a single audited output set, then adds metric-disagreement cases, bootstrap uncertainty, threshold sensitivity, and concept/cache integrity audits. Across the benchmark, output and appearance fidelity are at most weakly and inconsistently rank-correlated with decisive-mechanism distance for all three frozen SUTs, and a constructed positive control shows the estimator detects a known planted mechanism that output agreement misses, supporting decisive-evidence diagnostics for intelligent-vehicle simulation validation. All numerical claims are restricted to the declared exact-geometry benchmark, ten generators, and three frozen SUTs.

</details>

Abstract: Computer-use agents now solve most desktop tasks through direct software input, but a robot using the same computer must turn GUI knowledge into physical contact with a keyboard and mouse. We introduce ROSWorld, a benchmark that couples a physically simulated robot workstation to a live OSWorld desktop, so that a robot and a desktop agent pursue the same goals under the same official evaluators. The evaluated model receives observations only from the workstation camera views and operates the peripherals through either a single-arm rigid probe or dual-arm dexterous hands. Across five frontier models and 40 tasks in ten applications, models that complete 31-39 tasks on the desktop complete at most 23 with the probe and 28 with the hands. Paired trajectory analysis shows that models adapt their interaction strategies toward keyboard input, yet physical execution frequently fails through missed presses, incorrect targets, and unintended contacts. ROSWorld makes the gap between knowing what to do on a computer and physically doing it measurable.

</details>

Abstract: Frontier large language models (LLMs) have enabled vision-and-language navigation (VLN) through direct closed-loop perception and action. However, navigation performance depends strongly on the harness governing LLM-environment interactions, while existing harnesses largely rely on manually designed navigation tools, memory, and agent roles. We introduce NavRSI, a harness-centric recursive self-improvement framework that optimizes the navigation behavior of frozen LLMs through closed-loop experience. In each round, NavRSI iteratively collects navigation trajectories and failure diagnostics, employs a designer agent to rewrite the harness, and retains the revisions that prove effective, while the designer accumulates experience from previous revisions so that the harness and this experience co-evolve. Privileged simulator diagnostics guide only the designer and remain inaccessible to the navigating LLM, enabling deployment with a single RGB camera and primitive actions, without task-specific model training or additional navigation tools. Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements across LLMs from four vendors, with 85.64% success rate on the full R2R-CE val-unseen split with GPT-6-Astra, surpassing the previously reported best by 4.13%. Evaluations on RxR-CE, NavRAG-CE, and a real humanoid robot further demonstrate its generalizability.

</details>

Abstract: We present Ego4Gen (Egocentric 4D Future Generation), a world model for 4D future generation in robotic manipu lation. Given a single egocentric image and a text prompt, Ego4Gen jointly generates temporally aligned RGB frames, dense point maps, and dense scene flow, explicitly pre dicting the evolution of scene appearance, spatial struc ture, and motion. Built on Cosmos3-Nano, Ego4Gen ex tends the pretrained generative backbone through modality routed LoRA and asymmetric attention, using geometric pseudo-labels from MotionCrafter to learn to predict future geometry and motion latents. These latents can either be decoded into explicit 3D structure and motion or mapped to robot actions. To this end, we also introduce FlowIn verse, a lightweight inverse dynamics network trained in two stages to translate predicted 4D latents into robot ac tions. Experiments show that these geometry and motion latents outperform the evaluated latent-action and video latent baselines in out-of-distribution settings. Closed loop policy evaluations further demonstrate their utility for translating predicted 4D futures into robot control. To gether, these results support geometry-grounded motion to kens as a world-action interface, connecting 4D future gen eration with robotic manipulation and suggesting a path to ward reduced reliance on action-labeled data.

</details>

Abstract: Persistent monitoring of large-scale subsea infrastructure, such as offshore wind farm foundations, export cables, and inspection corridors, remains a fundamental challenge for autonomous underwater systems. The problem is not solely spatial coverage, but rather temporal coverage: ensuring that all relevant regions of interest are revisited within bounded time intervals under limited sensing, navigation uncertainty, and constrained vehicle resources. We compare three classes of trajectory strategies: (1) deterministic lane-based coverage (lawnmower patterns), (2) stochastic exploration (biased random walk with boundary constraints), and (3) coordinated partition-based strategies (Voronoi tessellation). Rather than seeking a universally superior method, we evaluate how varying levels of localization accuracy impact temporal coverage across two distinct operational profiles: persistent monitoring of the entire operational workspace versus targeted inspection of critical infrastructure. We present a simulation-based, quantitatively grounded study of temporal coverage in multi-AUV persistent monitoring, disentangling three interacting design dimensions: (i) trajectory generation strategy, (ii) fleet size, and (iii) localization accuracy provided by fixed acoustic beacon infrastructure. We introduce a cost-aware evaluation framework combining fleet size, number of beacons, and mission energy expenditure into a unified cost function. Results demonstrate that beyond a certain accuracy threshold, improvements in localization yield diminishing returns compared to coordinated trajectory design, an effect often obscured in integrated navigation-planning approaches. Coordinated-partition trajectories enable a reduced fleet size to achieve superior temporal coverage at a 39-46% lower overall life-cycle cost than rigid deterministic paths unoptimized for the localization infrastructure.

</details>

Abstract: General-purpose whole-body trackers can execute diverse humanoid motions, but they require dense reference trajectories. However, tasks usually specify only where, in which posture, and when the robot should arrive, leaving the motion in between to a separate planner. Inspired by human motor coordination, our key idea is that a low-level controller can organize the intervening motion and its pace by itself from sparse goals with arrival times. In this paper, we propose S-BFM, a novel behavioral foundation model superior in its autonomy: it generates whole-body transitions from target postures, poses, and arrival times alone. Specifically, we first introduce Spatial Grounding, which adds world-frame position and heading to a forward-backward behavior representation through spatial reconstruction. Then, Time-Conditioned Distillation is designed to learn transitions and pace from real motion through closed-loop imitation of a dense-reference teacher. Lastly, Generative Fine-Tuning is introduced to broaden the covered distances and arrival times with a distilled motion generator. In simulation, S-BFM lowers global joint-position error by over 32% relative to the best baseline under fixed and mixed horizons, and raises fixed-goal reach from 43.5% to 74.6% while cutting terminal position error from 1.67 to 0.26 m. We further deploy S-BFM on the full-size AGIBOT A3 humanoid. Sparse spatiotemporal goals thus suffice for natural, well-timed whole-body motion.

</details>

Abstract: Learning factories have been increasingly applied in engineering research and education. In this paper, we develop a small-scale, low-cost, fully open-sourced learning factory that leverages AI and digital twins to demonstrate smart and resilient manufacturing. The learning factory consists of an AGV, three robots, two conveyors and their digital twins. Three typical use cases are designed to demonstrate the capabilities of the learning factory, i.e., material sorting through computer vision-based robot control, coordinating AGV, robots and conveyors for assembly, and digital twin-driven predictive maintenance. The results show that the developed learning factory can adequately support the teaching and research activities on digital twins and AI on manufacturing. Its fully open-source architecture and relatively low deployment cost make it an ideal solution for universities and research institutions.

### [332] [Synthesizing Expressive Human-Object Interactions via Test-time Latent Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27603)

Abstract: When interacting with objects of unknown physical properties, humans sometimes probe, over-exert, stagger, and adjust through iterative adaptation instead of succeeding immediately. We argue that this process is an expressive but largely overlooked aspect of human-object interaction in motion synthesis. Both kinematic data-driven methods and physics-based controllers tend to produce flawlessly executed motions: the former lack sufficient data for learning fine-grained adaptation, while the latter use reinforcement learning (RL) and privileged object dynamics to learn robust, deterministic behaviors. We propose ExpHOI (Expressive Human-Object Interaction), a test-time latent adaptation framework built on an RL-trained physics-based control policy. During policy training, we encode diverse object dynamics contexts into a structured latent space. At test time, Advantage-Weighted Regression (AWR) iteratively optimizes the latent code for an object with unknown physical properties. Temporary mismatches between this code and the true object dynamics produce an expressive progression from tentative, error-prone trials to proficient interaction. Adaptation behaviors inevitably come with occasional failures, so a high-level planner is introduced to handle failed cases to maintain full, consistent animations. We demonstrate our framework's effectiveness on a large-object transportation task, where such adaptation is especially pronounced. To train our framework, we introduce a mass-diverse object dataset with rich physical-property annotations. Across carrying and push-pull, our method pairs over 90% success with markedly higher posture diversity, and is rated the most lifelike in our user study on animation liveliness.

</details>

Abstract: LiDAR-Inertial-Visual Odometry (LIVO) is a fundamental technology for autonomous systems; however, deployment on resource-constrained platforms remains challenging due to physical limitations. This paper presents Instant-LIVO, a tightly coupled, high-speed, real-time LIVO system. The system introduces a compact geometric map based on spatial-hashed OctVox and accelerates local-neighborhood retrieval and plane fitting through cache-assisted heuristic staged K-nearest-neighbor searches. It also employs a bounded single-reference-frame visual map that adaptively updates the reference frame according to the tracking status, while using Schmidt-type uncertainty-aware photometric fusion to maintain the correlation between the reference pose and the current state. The system performs multisensor fusion through a sequential process consisting of IMU prediction, LiDAR geometric correction, and visual photometric correction. It was evaluated on 32 public benchmarks and custom datasets. While maintaining competitive accuracy on both X86 and ARM platforms, Instant-LIVO achieves average processing-speed improvements of approximately 2.3x and 2.1x, respectively, and reduces peak memory usage to approximately 1/20 and 1/13, respectively. The results demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing LIO and LIVO baselines in both accuracy and computational efficiency.The implementation is available at: https://github.com/Faker-0714/Instant-LIVO.git.

</details>

Abstract: Driving models that reason may describe a hazard correctly while choosing their path for other reasons. We study where scene reasoning can enter an end-to-end planner so its influence can be checked. We freeze Drive-JEPA and use a fine-tuned vision-language model to state scene facts. Although fine-tuning improves fact accuracy, the two learned components we test show no detectable benefit from reading them. A rule-based fact check instead rejects or shortens paths that contradict the facts, linking each intervention to a stated fact. On the frozen planner alone, it gains 2.4 EPDMS points on navhard. Its 2.0-point margin over a control that changes the same frames without consulting facts remains statistically uncertain. A diagnostic using exact facts from maps and annotations yields a 3.6-point margin over its corresponding control, with a 95% confidence interval above zero. Separately, a path scorer and plan memory that read no stated facts raise navhard from 41.2 to 51.8. Bounding travel distance using the reasoner's facts adds 0.3 there and reduces navtest by 2.0. The fact check makes each intervention auditable, while the driving value of the reasoner's facts remains unresolved.

</details>

Abstract: Latent world models plan by rolling out hundreds of candidate action sequences through a transformer predictor, so a single decision can take tens of seconds. Existing accelerations prune spatial tokens as in vision-language models, yet a sampling-based planner uses predicted costs only to rank candidates, so a rollout can be compressed freely as long as the ranking survives. We propose SOAR, a training-free method that adapts rollout compression to each planning decision. At every decision, SOAR probes a few candidates under several token budgets and keeps the smallest budget that preserves their ranking, so it compresses as far as the current decision allows, without task labels. A closed-loop study of more than 80,000 episodes fixes the rest of the rollout: the first step, which alone reads a real observation, stays exact, and the context frame is cached rather than recomputed. On seven navigation and manipulation tasks with frozen checkpoints, SOAR matches dense-level planning success at 2.3x speedup and retains 98% at 2.5x. Our code is available at https://github.com/starrywiki/SOAR.

</details>

Abstract: Training a humanoid motion tracker by backpropagating imagined returns through a learned world model is fast, because the model gives a first-order policy gradient. We ask how much of that gradient holds in the simulator. On a tracker for the Unitree G1, the $0.5\%$ of imagined rollouts with the largest gradients carry $22\%$ of the summed gradient norm, yet neither a second rollout from the same state nor the simulator confirms them. When the actions are moved along the model's gradient, the simulator returns 94% of the predicted change in return where the gradient norm is below the batch median, 48% between 3 and 10 times the median, and 1% above 30 times; the part of a gradient that either check confirms stops growing at a few times the median. We therefore cap each rollout's action-gradient norm at 10 times the batch median, draw imagination starts in proportion to their predicted gradient norm so that the cap removes less, and reset episodes where the world model and critic predict the lowest return. Trained on 223 hours of motion and tested on 353 motions that no actor performed in training, the method raises the normalized return of SuperMimic, the baseline it builds on, from 0.953 to 0.967 and the lowest $1\%$ of test clips from 0.65 to 0.76, halves the test clips that fall persistently, from 15--18 to 7--10, and reaches the baseline's final return in 40% of the baseline's iterations and half its training time. On LAFAN1, trained and evaluated on the same clips as prior work, it reaches 0.935 against 0.906.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language model (VLM)-based agents offer a promising approach to robotic manipulation, yet remain limited by inadequate memory organization, uncertain failure attribution, and limited reuse of execution experience. We propose Embodied-Harness, a dual-agent framework that improves manipulation by refining the execution harness without updating model parameters. The Actor interacts with the environment through a robot-control interface, while the Reflector converts execution evidence into reusable operational guidance. Hierarchical Memory Architecture maintains short-term execution context and long-term experiential knowledge to support execution and reflection. Evidence-based Experience Reflection grounds failure diagnosis in observed interactions, while Recursive Harness Self-Improvement refines operational guidance across successive attempts. Experiments on RoboTwin 2.0, RoboSPA, and RMBench demonstrate the effectiveness of Embodied-Harness in generalization under environmental variations, spatial reasoning, long-horizon planning, and memory-dependent manipulation. Our method achieves success rates of 82.4%, 76.7%, and 94.4% on the three benchmarks, respectively. Real-world evaluation further yields an average success rate of 96.7% across three manipulation tasks. These results highlight the potential of Embodied-Harness to improve robotic manipulation through experience-driven harness self-improvement.

</details>

Abstract: Linear manufacturing typically operates in highly structured settings with standardized objects, allowing robots to execute pre-programmed motions based on precise models. In contrast, circular manufacturing involves dynamic and less controlled environments, where product components can vary due to appearance and physical changes across different product generations. In this context, uncertainty arises from both the unknown or partially observed states of components (e.g., damaged surfaces, altered shapes) and the unstructured disassembly environment, often characterized by heavy clutter, occlusions, and limited sensing viewpoints. This demands a flexible and adaptable robotic grasping system that can cope with unexpected object changes. To address these challenges, this paper proposes an autonomous grasp learning framework designed for highly uncertain and variable conditions. The framework explicitly distinguishes between uncertainty due to noisy or incomplete sensory observations (aleatoric uncertainty) and uncertainty due to limited knowledge and distribution shift (epistemic uncertainty). Moreover, the framework employs an active perception strategy to reduce visual ambiguity: semantic and spatial scene understanding is achieved using vision language models (VLMs), while view planning enables the robot to adjust its viewpoint to improve observability. In addition, the approach leverages large-scale human demonstration data to learn interaction patterns, which are then transferred to robotic end-effectors, enabling robust manipulation in these variable settings. Quantitative validation, including the utility of active vision on disassembled components in practice, is expected to form an important part of future work.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon embodied tasks require agents to ground instructions in changing observations, coordinate heterogeneous tools, and recover from failures that can propagate through downstream operations. Although existing LLM-based agents retain experience and refine reusable skills, successful local repairs do not by themselves establish complete-task gains over the current shared procedure library. We present EvoTER, an experience-driven framework that enables embodied agents to self-evolve tool-use skills without updating model parameters. EvoTER operates through a three-role evolution loop: Explorer records tool-use trajectories; Diagnoser distills successful and failed executions into skills for tool selection, parameter computation, postcondition checks, and recovery; and Validator retains candidate revisions only when they improve complete-task success in fresh sessions. In simulation, EvoTER improves overall task success from a pre-evolution baseline of 53.60% to 80.40%. In real-world experiments, simulation initialization reduces the attempts required to acquire short-horizon skills, while continued real-world evolution improves long-horizon success from 20% to 80%-100% across tasks and enables the evolving skill library to incorporate new tool functionality introduced after simulation development. Project page: https://evoter-project.github.io/

Abstract: Frontier models have demonstrated remarkable general-purpose reasoning capabilities, opening new possibilities for intelligent decision making in the physical world. In autonomous driving, many recent approaches seek to leverage these capabilities through additional adaptation for action output, particularly in scenarios where existing models underperform. We introduce Generalist Action Critic (GAC), a zero-shot framework that combines frontier models' general-purpose reasoning with the planning competence of specialized driving models through an inference-time harness. In GAC, specialized planners generate multiple candidate trajectories and make an initial selection, while a frozen frontier model serves as an action critic that assesses the selected trajectory against scene evidence and either retains it or reselects from the available candidates. The harness combines camera images with spatial overlays, provides driving context and reasoning principles through textual instructions, and regulates the information available at each stage to support trajectory assessment and reselection. On HUGSIM, GAC raises DrivoR's HD-Score to 43.2, achieving state-of-the-art performance among methods trained without additional simulation data. GAC also consistently improves closed-loop driving safety across three planners, with relative HD-Score gains of up to 32.1%. Experiments on NAVSIM v1 further reveal how GAC improves planning performance by correcting up to 52.13% of eligible unsafe trajectory selections while introducing new failures in only 0.67% of originally high-quality selections. These results demonstrate the potential of harnessing frontier models to improve driving safety through inference-time cooperation with specialized planners.

</details>

Abstract: Forward-looking sonar (FLS) provides reliable underwater perception where optical sensing is limited, but its use for navigation is hindered by the unobserved elevation of each return. As a result, motion estimated directly from sonar images suffers from an unknown metric scale that is commonly resolved using a Doppler velocity log (DVL). We present BRINA (Bottom-Referenced Inertial Navigation with AKAZE), a sonar-inertial SLAM system for marine vehicles that uses the FLS as its only exteroceptive sensor. BRINA estimates the sonar altitude from the first seabed return in each beam and uses it to project matched features onto the seabed, providing metric odometry without a DVL. We further derive a bound relating the remaining scale error to seabed tilt and slope variability. The method is evaluated in simulation with an autonomous underwater vehicle, and in field experiments with uncrewed surface vehicles over ten missions, using two FLS systems under different operating conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Humanoid robots can reduce personnel exposure in electrical switching, but safe control is challenging because the tool must reach a target switch next to similar devices, where an imprecise operator command can contact a non-target switch. This study proposes a runtime safety filter that checks each operator command during virtual-reality teleoperation of a humanoid robot. The filter integrates a scene model estimated from the robot head camera and a target-conditioned control barrier function quadratic program that keeps the tool away from the non-target switch while passing safe commands unchanged. In real-world experiments on a Unitree G1 humanoid robot, with six rounds at each of two standing positions, target operations succeed in all ten rounds with an unchanged target and no non-target actuation. A simulation study achieves 9/9 safe completions, compared with 5/9 for raw teleoperation, demonstrating the feasibility of the method for humanoid switching operations.

</details>


### [343] [Interactive Semi-Parametric On-Track Learning for Vehicle Dynamics in Autonomous Racing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27932)
*Jungbin Kim,Youngim Nam,Cheolhyeon Kwon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Autonomous racing requires an accurate vehicle dynamics model near the limits of handling, yet a mismatch between the nominal and actual dynamics inevitably remains. To reduce this mismatch, existing methods learn from on-track data, i.e., data collected while the vehicle drives on the track under racing conditions, by adapting the physical parameters of the nominal model or by learning a residual that captures the remaining mismatch. However, these on-track learning methods typically handle either the physical parameters or the residual separately, which restricts their ability to resolve the mismatch. To address this limitation, we propose an interactive semi-parametric learning framework that learns both the physical parameters and the residual under a single shared prediction-error objective. Since updating one changes the learning target of the other, they are recursively updated in turn, each guided by the other's latest update. Each update is then accepted only if it does not increase the shared objective, so that the objective is guaranteed to be non-increasing and convergent over the recursion. Numerical simulations and hardware experiments show that the proposed framework outperforms the baselines. Compared with the nominal model, it reduces the one-step prediction errors of lateral velocity and yaw rate by 90.0% and 81.6% in simulation and by 54.0% and 59.4% on hardware.

</details>


### [344] [Real-time neuromorphic audio and vision perception for autonomous robotic applications: Integration and experimental evaluation on a rover platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27953)

Abstract: Neuromorphic sensing and computing offer a promising alternative to conventional deep learning pipelines for mobile robots, where perception must run continuously under tight latency and energy constraints. However, most neuromorphic solutions are still evaluated offline or on isolated benchmarks, and their integration into complete robotic systems remains largely unexplored. In this technical report, a four-wheel rover platform is presented that combines three real-time perception pipelines: a classical audio tagging pipeline based on Pretrained Audio Neural Networks (PANNs) running on an NVIDIA Jetson GPU, a neuromorphic audio pipeline in which a spiking neural network (SNN) is deployed on the SynSense Xylo Audio~2 chip, and a neuromorphic vision pipeline in which events from an event-based camera are processed by the SpikeYOLO spiking detector for traffic cone detection. On the Xylo chip, the whole processing chain---from the microphone signal to the classification of low- and high-pitched sounds---is performed on-chip, while the host only configures the device and reads the output neurons. All models are deployed as containerised services and integrated through ROS~2, and a configurable action dispatcher maps the detected classes to robot behaviours, closing the loop between perception and actuation. The platform is demonstrated in a proof-of-concept scenario in which the robot reacts to sound classes detected by both audio pipelines and detects traffic cones with the event camera, showing the feasibility of integrating neuromorphic hardware into a multimodal robotic platform operating in real time.

</details>


### [345] [What Must a Model Tell a Planner? Outcome Interfaces for Risk-Aware Planning After Robot Damage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27939)
*Hassan Iqbal,Krishna Kumar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: When a robot is damaged, it must decide which routes it can still afford, and the amount of risk it may accept can change from one mission to the next. We argue that whether a learned model helps such a robot depends on its decision interface, that is, on what the model hands to the planner. A decision policy tells the planner what to do, whereas an outcome model, a kind of world model, predicts the consequences of each route. We study simulated Unitree Go2 quadrupeds and H1 humanoids with weakened motors, added payloads, or reduced foot friction. We compare four interfaces. The first is a decision policy, and the second is the same policy trained with fixed penalties for infeasible routes. The third is an outcome model that predicts the feasibility and travel time of each route. The fourth, which we call a capability contract, is an outcome model that predicts the chance of success and the travel time of the robot on each terrain and composes them along a route. All share the same collected experience, matched tuning and risk calibration, and an uncertainty guard. Two comparatively low-risk probes let the capability contract rank hazardous terrains about as well as four new trials on every hazard. At a calibrated 5% limit on selecting empirically infeasible routes, outcome models cut declined missions from about 32% to 7-8% on Go2 and from 57% to 35-36% on H1 compared with the plain policy. Fixed penalties narrow this gap, but the best penalty changes with the risk target. Predicting outcomes lets the planner preserve missions by changing routes as risk requirements tighten.

</details>

Abstract: Dexterous hand design demands repeated expert work across geometry, robot-description code, collision debugging, grasp initialization, and policy training. We present Task2Hand, a Skill: a specification package that turns a functional manipulation brief into a parameterized hand model and routes every candidate through cost-ordered contact, dynamics, and learning gates. Each gate returns a status and an evidence record; on failure, the Skill records the earliest blocker, revises one bounded parameter group, and reruns the cheaper gates. The agent generates, validates, and trains autonomously within declared bounds, and escalates trade-offs, plateaus, and conflicting evidence to a human who sets the next design direction. An evidence-driven design trajectory for bidirectional in-hand manipulation yielded SAGE Hand (Skill-Aware Generative Embodiment): four fingers, 16 actuated DoF, an integrated guiding palm, compact roots, cylindrical tips, and a collision-cleared opposable thumb. Under a matched training protocol, SAGE Hand reaches 45.6% task success on a hexagonal prism against 37.3% for the LEAP hand, and 70.8% on a bicolor cylinder against 51.6%. Dense simulated tactile input more than halves the cylinder drop rate. In a controlled iteration study, the Skill path preserves capture at 164/165 trials while the path without the Skill degrades to 14/165, confirming that the gates act as a regression guard during refinement. Accepted candidates pack with URDF/MJCF, metrics, checkpoints, and a provenance manifest.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we first study the problem of finding a state-space realization of a given transfer function that adheres to a given connectivity pattern (i.e., support). While variants of this problem have been studied in distributed and structured control, existing work typically focuses on connectivity patterns relating to some networked plant. In contrast, we are interested in arbitrary connectivity patterns, which are relevant to modeling problems in sensorimotor neuroscience. We show that this problem is equivalent to solving a system of multivariate polynomial equations, and can be solved exactly without relaxation or approximation. We then study the reverse problem: given a connectivity pattern, what transfer functions have a state-space realization that adheres to this pattern? In other words, what transfer functions can this pattern realize? A surprising finding is that any given pattern can realize nearly all transfer functions, or very few transfer functions. We provide rigorous results and algorithms for both problems, leveraging technical machinery from algebraic geometry. The efficacy of our approach is demonstrated in examples at the end of the paper.

</details>

Abstract: The growing share of DC-based generation, storage and loads has increased interest in DC microgrids. As a result, DC-bus voltage regulation and stability have become active research topics, with considerable effort directed at improving the transient performance of conventional droop control. Although numerous variants of droop algorithms have been proposed, many rely on increasingly complex control structures with multiple tuning parameters and measurements, thereby increasing the commissioning burden. Moreover, stable interaction with the surrounding microgrid is not guaranteed by design. To address these issues, this paper develops a passivity-constrained $\mathcal{H}_\infty$ synthesis method for the DC-bus voltage controller. The proposed $\mathcal{H}_\infty$ controller emulates the desired grid-forming behavior of the converter by minimizing the $\mathcal{H}_\infty$ error norm between device-level performance indices and their desired values, while additionally enforcing passivity during the optimization. The novel $\mathcal{H}_\infty$ control scheme is benchmarked in the frequency domain against representative droop-based and virtual-DC-machine algorithms from the literature, while experiments on a four-converter DC microgrid corroborate the frequency-domain analysis and validate the practical applicability of the algorithm.

</details>

Abstract: Learning for optimal power flow (OPF) has recently been advocated to accelerate OPF solutions by using a pre-trained neural network (NN) to predict directly from given inputs such as the load demand. However, existing learning-for-OPF methods largely fall short of ensuring the optimality and feasibility of their predictions. They also struggle to adapt at inference time to unseen topology scenarios, such as those caused by line or generator outages. This work puts forth Loop-OPF, a solver-informed NN that builds its layers from the iterations of the primal-dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) method. Under standard convexity and regularity conditions on DC-OPF, the resultant fixed-point output of Loop-OPF is thus optimal and feasible. To speed up PDHG across diverse OPF scenarios, Loop-OPF learns certain PDHG parameters in a self-supervised manner, without any OPF solution labels. We adopt the deep equilibrium (DEQ) framework for training so that the memory cost does not grow with the number of layers. As the grid topology enters every layer as an input, the same trained Loop-OPF directly adapts to unseen contingencies without retraining. Numerical tests on the IEEE 300-bus and PEGASE 1354-, 2869-, and 9241-bus networks have demonstrated the accuracy and topology adaptivity of the proposed Loop-OPF over competing learning-for-OPF methods.

</details>

Abstract: Urban mixed traffic networks provide diverse mobility options, yet conventional routing guidance with fixed travel modes leaves the benefits of coordinating travel modes and route planning largely unexploited. This paper proposes an integrated mode recommendation and dynamic routing guidance framework for urban mixed traffic networks comprising private vehicles, autonomous vehicles, and buses. We formulate a dynamic trip index (DTX) that incorporates mode-specific travel time, monetary cost, and convenience, enabling consistent evaluation of trip quality across travel modes. Using this measure, we develop a mode recommendation method that selects feasible autonomous vehicle or bus services based on predicted DTX for travelers without private vehicles, while preserving service capacity for travelers with known demand. We integrate this method with dynamic routing guidance for private and autonomous vehicles to improve the average trip quality of all travelers. Simulation studies involving $1{,}500$ travelers on a real-world road network in Shanghai, China, show that the proposed framework increases the mean realized DTX by $13.01\%$ compared with dynamic routing guidance alone and by $19.68\%$ compared with fixed travel modes with preplanned routes, highlighting the value of mode flexibility in improving trip quality beyond route adaptation.

</details>

Abstract: Grid operators now require dynamic models of data centers and other large computational loads, and new ride-through rules assume that these models predict how facilities respond to disturbances. This review asks what evidence supports that assumption. We coded 160 documents, screened from 1,424 de-duplicated database and seed records plus 65 added documents, by the strongest reference object each presents, from grid-side records to simulation only. Across 19 field-evidence entries, 15 requirement documents from three continents and nine model classes, we find two comparisons of a standard library model with measured data-center events: with default parameters the model failed both, and refitting it to each event separately reduced the error but was never tested on an unseen event. Among the sources reviewed, we found no successful held-out validation of a frozen, facility-specific data-center model against an independent measured disturbance, and 25 of the 33 model-phenomenon combinations with any evidence involve no electrical measurement. The features that distinguish the new rules (memory of repeated dips, recovery within 0.5-3 s, proportional power reduction during the sag, overvoltage response) are those current phasor models cannot represent or have never been tested against. In the operator procedures examined, simulations and model-to-model benchmarks are accepted as evidence of model fidelity, unlike the measured validation required of generators, and proposed mitigations have mostly been evaluated on unvalidated facility models. We propose context-of-use gates that separate calibration from validation, release labels for model claims, a minimum validation protocol with acceptance criteria, and a research agenda.

</details>

Abstract: This paper investigates strong functional observability, strong functional detectability, and strong$^*$ functional detectability for continuous-time linear systems with unknown inputs in both the state and measurement equations. Extending Kratz's finite algebraic approach \cite{26}, zero-output trajectories are characterized through the projected kernel of a finite matrix, without differentiating the unknown inputs. This characterization yields a necessary and sufficient rank condition for strong functional observability. For strong functional detectability, a frequency-dependent rank criterion involving only the original system matrices is derived, along with an equivalent finite test based on reduced zero-output dynamics. Strong$^*$ functional detectability is then characterized by an additional algebraic rank condition, and its relationship with the existence of stable functional observers is examined. A constructive observer design procedure based on constrained Sylvester equations is also developed. Particular cases and a numerical process example illustrate the theoretical results.

</details>

Abstract: The growth of Low-Altitude Air Transport (LAAT) systems calls for effective macroscopic modeling tools to manage dense drone traffic. The Macroscopic Fundamental Diagram (MFD) offers a promising framework for this purpose, but its conventional formulations do not directly account for the three-dimensional interactions and path deviations that characterize drone operations. To address this challenge, we introduce a projected-space framework that represents drone motion using projected speed and shortest origin-destination (OD) distances, eliminating the dependence on realized trip lengths that are unknown at trip generation. Under suitable assumptions, we prove the steady-state exactness of the projected-space accumulation-based model and establish deterministic finite-window error bounds for the trip-based estimator. The projected-space accumulation-based formulation is further extended to heterogeneous multi-class systems to accommodate different drone characteristics and operational priorities. Microscopic simulations validate the projected-space formulations under stationary and transient traffic conditions, including hysteresis. Moreover, simulations involving heterogeneous priority classes demonstrate that mixed-priority operations can improve network outflow under the investigated scenarios. These findings establish a theoretical foundation for projected-space macroscopic modeling of LAAT systems and support its application to real-time traffic estimation and future airspace management and control.

</details>

Abstract: The active learning of real control systems requires informative roll-outs of controls. In this paper, we present a local optimization algorithm that finds the trajectory that most reduces uncertainty about the flow of a control system modeled by a Gaussian process. Therefore, we derive an approximation scheme for the posterior kernel of the Gaussian process after observing it on an entire continuous-time trajectory and establish a Pontryagin-type necessary condition for the optimizer. As the flow is unknown, the actual trajectory can deviate from the planned trajectory. We propose a trajectory planner that searches for the most informative control within a maximal allowed deviation horizon instead of a fixed time horizon. We show by trajectory-wise time rescaling that the underlying optimal control problem is equivalent to a fixed end time optimal control problem. Finally, we study both the fixed end time planner and the fixed maximum deviation planner in a numerical example and compare them with benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: This paper investigates linear quadratic (LQ) control for discrete-time stochastic systems to balance control performance and actuation rate. In our framework, the control input is sparse in time, and the actuation timings are determined in an event-based manner. We examine two types of problems: A sparsity-promoting regularization problem and a hard-constraint problem with a prescribed average actuation rate. Due to the combinatorial nature of these problems, finding optimal solutions is generally hard. Inspired by rollout algorithms in the context of dynamic programming, we develop a rollout-based approach to obtain tractable suboptimal policies. We show closed-loop stability and establish theoretical performance guarantees with respect to classical periodic control. Moreover, under additional conditions, we quantify the performance improvement over periodic control and prove that this improvement is strictly positive. A numerical example demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed method in balancing control performance and actuation rate.

</details>

Abstract: We propose an incentive-based fleet rebalancing scheme for vehicle-sharing systems that uses fare discounts to steer users toward alternative pick-up locations. The discounts solve an optimal control problem that trades off users leaving the service against incentive costs over a day of operation. Users' responses to incentives are modeled as a Nash equilibrium of logit models coupled through shared vehicle-availability constraints, which makes the control problem a mathematical program with nested equilibrium constraints. We develop a first-order algorithm that backpropagates through the equilibria via nonsmooth implicit differentiation and enjoys convergence guarantees. In a case study that uses Helsinki bike-sharing data, our scheme reduces the number of leaving users by up to 99% relative to no incentives, and outperforms heuristic policies while using up to 52% less incentive.

</details>

Abstract: AI data centers are an emerging electricity demand, but limited local grid capacity could delay their connection and expansion. While existing operational strategies consider spatio-temporal workload flexibility, few studies address the strategic sizing and allocation of diverse accelerator archetypes with different capital and energy-efficiency trade-offs. This paper unlocks the flexibility potential of heterogeneous accelerators by jointly sizing and allocating different AI workloads (training, delay-tolerant batch work, and online inference requests) across sites and hours to meet the time-varying grid constraint at minimum cost. Workload-specific throughput and energy coefficients link these decisions to facility power. In a representative two-site case, heterogeneous sizing reduces daily cost by 6.5% relative to spatio-temporal scheduling with fixed capacity. When evaluated using workload-specific performance, a capacity design based on workload-averaged hardware coefficients costs 7.7% more than the proposed design. The proposed design also eliminates the envelope violations of an unconstrained design at a 0.25\% daily cost increase. These results show how hardware heterogeneity and workload flexibility jointly shape economical, grid-compliant capacity plans.

</details>

Abstract: Formation control based on geometric constraints often requires either rigid interaction graphs or a common orientation reference. This paper investigates whether a prescribed planar formation can instead be achieved using only $N-1$ pairwise interactions while preserving global rotational freedom. To this end, a guiding-vector-field-inspired framework is developed in which each selected distance relation is represented by a parameterized circular manifold and the remaining geometric freedom is coordinated through relative virtual phases, based on which, a distributed controller is then constructed. The closed-loop system is shown to possess only the desired equilibrium set and a degenerate undesired equilibrium set corresponding to collapse of the central edge. The latter is proved to form a saddle-type equilibrium manifold with a measure-zero stable set, yielding almost-global convergence to the desired formation. In addition, the controller admits an implementation under mutually misaligned local frames using locally expressed relative measurements and scalar phase information.

</details>

Abstract: Upper-mid-band spectrum offers bandwidth opportunities for the integrated networks of ground vehicles, unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs), and low Earth orbit (LEO) satellites to support 6G wide-area vehicle-to-everything (V2X) connectivity. However, V2X services' latency and reliability are sensitive to the mid-band selection due to frequency-dependent propagation and rain attenuation. This paper investigates environment-aware spectrum agility over candidate frequency range 3 (FR3) bands within 7.125-24.25 GHz, jointly with end-to-end (E2E) multi-path traffic splitting and transmit power control. Each data packet is partitioned into non-overlapping subflows over direct and UAV-assisted paths, with failed sky-link subflows selectively retransmitted subject to retransmission and freshness constraints. We formulate latency- and reliability-oriented problems, followed by a unified sequential optimization problem incorporating band-switching costs and average quality-of-service (QoS) violation budgets. To coordinate optimization decisions across heterogeneous timescales, we develop an agentic AI-assisted hierarchical multi-agent reinforcement learning (AA-HMARL) framework. A high-level agentic supervisor integrates recurrent memory, analytical evaluation of candidate bands, and event-triggered replanning to coordinate slow environmental adaptation with fast channel and retransmission dynamics, while distributed ground vehicles and UAV agents optimize data splitting and power using constrained MAPPO. Simulation results demonstrate an adjustable reliability-latency trade-off. Particularly, AA-HMARL reduces the mean E2E completion latency and the mean number of retransmission rounds among successfully delivered updates by 17.8-25.5% and 57.6-61.5%, respectively, and reduces the mean age of information (AoI) by 12.7-14.6% relative to fixed-band MAPPO across two traffic types.

</details>

Abstract: We establish two complementary results on common strictly positive real (SPR) numerators for Hurwitz-stable interval polynomial families. First, for every order $n \ge 10$, we construct a monic Hurwitz-stable interval polynomial family with rational endpoints and positive width in every nonleading coefficient such that every nonzero real numerator of degree at most $n$ produces a strictly negative real part at some member and some positive frequency. In particular, robust Hurwitz stability alone does not guarantee equal-degree SPR synthesis in high orders. Second, for every order $n \ge 1$, we prove that a common real SPR numerator of exact degree $n$ always exists whenever the lower endpoint of each interior coefficient interval is at most $10^{-5}$ times its upper endpoint, while the constant and leading coefficients vary over arbitrary compact positive intervals.

</details>

Abstract: Co-optimizing energy and flexible ramping reserves may dispatch generators at locational marginal prices (LMPs) below their submitted energy offer prices, necessitating out-of-market make-whole payments. Such payments create discriminatory settlements among generators and may incentivize strategic bidding for higher profits. This paper proposes LMP+U, an in-market uniform price adder to LMP for transmission-constrained networks, which minimizes demand payments subject to zero out-of-market make-whole uplift, preserves LMP-based congestion charges, and provides locally truthful bidding incentives for price-taking generators. We also identify conditions under which a price-taking generator can increase its profit by inflating its energy offer under conventional LMP with make-whole uplift. Numerical simulations illustrate the properties of LMP+U and its advantages over conventional LMP in congested networks.

</details>

Abstract: One key challenge in distribution system state es- timation is unknown input statistics from the growing inte- gration of distributed energy resources (DERs), which may largely deviate from Gaussian uncertainty. Existing model-based estimators often rely on the assumption that all inputs follow known Gaussian distributions, while data-driven methods suffer from overfitting and failure in out-of-distribution scenarios. This paper proposes a Bayesian bias learning (BBL)-enabled probabilistic voltage estimation method in distribution systems with unknown DER generation statistics. The proposed learning- enabled method consists of two stages: probabilistic uncertainty inference and computationally efficient voltage estimation. First, a physics-constrained probabilistic density inference network is developed to learn Gaussian mixture representations of DER generation from historical data. Then, the resulting probabilistic representations, treated as pseudo-measurements, are integrated into a BBL-enabled voltage estimator via a decomposition- and-parallelization mechanism. Furthermore, utilizing a linear analytical solution, the proposed BBL-enabled voltage estimator efficiently captures linearization-induced biases and attains ac- curate probabilistic estimation. By integrating learning-enabled probabilistic uncertainty modeling into the parallel estimation structure, the proposed method achieves uncertainty-aware scal- able voltage estimation. Case studies on two unbalanced IEEE distribution networks show that the proposed estimator achieves significantly higher accuracy and computational efficiency under unknown uncertainty, outperforming recent data-driven and model-based methods.

</details>

Abstract: Small modular reactors (SMRs) are being considered as compact, high-energy-density power sources for future all-electric ships. In such systems, propulsion-load variations can propagate through the electrical network, turbine-generator set, steam generator, and reactor, producing coupled nuclear, thermal, mechanical, and electrical transients. This paper presents an integrated dynamic load-following analysis of an SMR-powered all-electric ship with and without battery energy storage system (BESS) support. The model couples an integral pressurized water reactor, a once-through moving-boundary steam generator, a simplified turbine model, a synchronous generator, an AC shipboard electrical network, an asynchronous propulsion motor, and a grid-connected BESS. A propulsion-load reduction is applied through the motor torque command, and the responses of the SMR-alone and SMR+BESS configurations are compared. The results show that, without BESS support, the sudden decrease in propulsion motor power creates a temporary surplus between turbine mechanical power and electrical demand, causing a large over-frequency transient and rapid steam-cycle adjustment. With BESS support, the battery absorbs excess electrical power during the transient, reducing the frequency peak from approximately 61.1 Hz to about 60.06 Hz. The BESS also indirectly smooths the upstream steam-cycle and reactor responses, including steam flow, steam-generator pressure, feedwater flow, coolant temperature, reactivity, reactor thermal power, and steam-generator region lengths. These results demonstrate the importance of modeling nuclear-powered electric ships as coupled multi-domain systems and show that BESS integration can improve short-term load-following performance.

</details>

### [365] [FM4SAR-Bench 1.0: Towards A Fair Benchmark for Evaluating Foundation Models for SAR Image Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27158)

Abstract: Foundation models have advanced SAR image interpretation but created a heterogeneous, fragmented model ecosystem. Existing models differ substantially in their input, training data, objectives, architectures, and release policy, yet the field lacks a unified taxonomy and comparative framework. Current evaluations rely primarily on task-specific benchmarks, which are ill-suited to fair comparison among models with fundamentally different input-output formats and task paradigms, and often overlook data transparency, reproducibility, interpretability, and deployment readiness. Limited SAR data accessibility also means high reported performance does not guarantee access to training data, code, or weights, limiting reproduction, benchmarking, and deployment. To address these issues, we propose a systematic taxonomy and hierarchical evaluation framework for SAR-related foundation models: five rating levels, six dimensions, and fine-grained criteria, with separate protocols for VFMs and VLMs. Beyond overall ratings, it yields interpretable multidimensional capability profiles and supports user-defined weighting, so rankings follow user priorities. Utilizing a fixed auxiliary multimodal large language model in combination with human review, we conduct a systematic meta-analysis and multidimensional assessment of 76 VFMs and 40 VLMs. In terms of SAR benchmark task performance under our framework, SAR-native VLMs do not significantly outperform remote sensing VLMs pretrained on mixed multimodal corpora in which SAR is one of several modalities. This lack of clear advantage is largely due to data availability: large-scale, high-quality, public annotated SAR data remain scarce, restricting both model and training-data scale for SAR-native models. The framework provides a structured basis for requirement-aware model selection and remains extensible as new models emerge.

</details>

Abstract: The number of structures fluorescence microscopy can image in one sample is limited by how few fluorophores can be separated without significant spectral overlap. Semantic unmixing circumvents this limit by imaging two structures in one fluorescent channel and computationally separating the superimposed image into one image per structure. Because pixels rarely identify their structure, unmixing requires both local and long-range spatial context. The relative brightness of the two structures is unknown and can be very uneven, so predictions must remain accurate across mixing severities, from balanced mixtures to ones where a structure is barely visible. Existing methods either process small image patches or add context through auxiliary pathways that slow inference on large images. We present MambaSplit, a hierarchical state-space encoder with a convolutional decoder that captures context across large tiles in linear time and predicts both channels in one deterministic pass. MambaSplit trains either on computationally summed channel images with random mixing ratios, where a lightweight head estimates the unknown ratio and conditions the decoder on it, or on experimentally acquired superpositions. Across five datasets and nine mixing ratios, MambaSplit achieves the highest RI-PSNR, by up to 3.0 dB, in 11 of 15 dataset-severity combinations and ties in one more, while running 5.9x faster than the strongest baseline. On acquired superpositions, fine-tuning a model pretrained on summed inputs matches or exceeds all baselines. Ablations show that accuracy and robustness come mainly from the training recipe: large tiles, mixing ratios sampled across their full range (up to 11 dB over fixed-ratio training), and independently drawn channel crops (3.9 dB on BioSR). The state-space backbone makes large tiles affordable, enabling high-content screening and event-driven smart microscopy.

</details>


### [367] [A High-Compression Transformer VAE for Efficient and Controllable 3D Medical Image Generation with Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26907)

Abstract: Many medical image processing tasks, such as completing missing regions or correcting acquisition artifacts, can be cast as volumetric generation conditioned on observations of the same patient. Diffusion Transformers offer scalable capacity and flexible conditioning for this setting, but they operate on a learned image representation that must preserve fine anatomical and pathological detail while keeping complete three-dimensional volumes tractable. We bring Transformer variational autoencoding and multimodal diffusion together to address this challenge. Trained on BrainTumor-256K, a 249M Transformer VAE encodes and decodes $256^3$ volumes 7.6x faster than a comparably sized CNN trained on the same collection. Their PSNR difference is near zero with training-sized crop inputs, but the Transformer leads by 0.749 dB with full-volume inputs and an unchanged scoring region. Increasing Transformer capacity to 499M improves PSNR across all four brain MRI evaluation sets while retaining 3.8x lower encoding-decoding latency than the CNN. Fast encoding also allows conditions to be constructed dynamically in image space during latent diffusion training. Within the compressed representation, a shared Diffusion Transformer integrates text and spatial references for volumetric synthesis, completion and restoration. The brain-trained autoencoder further transfers to non-brain MRI and CT without target-domain fine-tuning, extending its use beyond the anatomy and modality on which it was learned. The framework provides a reusable representation and conditioning interface for efficient, controllable medical image generation. Code is available at https://github.com/OncoMMAI/brainmri-vit-vae3d.

</details>

Abstract: Particle picking is a key step in single-particle cryo-electron microscopy. It is a challenging task where tomographic projections of proteins and other macromolecules are identified in extremely noisy images. In this work, we introduce a particle picker based on a seminorm defined in terms of a fast wavelet approximation to the earth mover's distance. Our picker is template-free, self-supervised and does not require any prior knowledge of the molecules of interest other than their approximate size. We test it on three public datasets and show that it achieves state-of-the-art reconstructions.

</details>

Abstract: Task-oriented image signal processors (ISPs) adapt RAW-to-RGB processing for downstream perception. However, explicit demosaicing is often simplified, kept fixed, or optimized primarily for reconstruction quality, leaving this stage insufficiently integrated into task adaptation. We propose \textbf{Dem2TA}, a framework that incorporates fidelity-supervised demosaicing into the adaptation of the perception pipeline. Starting from a pretrained demosaicing model, we jointly adapt the reconstruction frontend and downstream network using reconstruction and task supervision, together with complementary semantic and task representation alignment. We then freeze the adapted frontend and jointly optimize a lightweight local ISP with the downstream model to refine task-specific rendering without changing the reconstructed linear RGB. Experiments on synthetic RAW detection and box-prompted segmentation demonstrate the effectiveness of representation-guided joint adaptation and subsequent local rendering. Evaluation on a manually verified real-capture dataset further supports transfer beyond synthetic inputs. Compared with TA-ISP on the same adapted foundation, Dem2TA improves detection AP by 0.46 points and segmentation IoU by 1.06 points on synthetic COCO RAW, and segmentation IoU by 0.63 points on real captures.

</details>


### [370] [Learned Score-Based Turbo Message Passing for Compressive Image Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27337)
*Peihang Li,Hao Jiang,Xiaojun Yuan*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Score-based generative models have been incorpo-rated into message passing algorithms for compressive image recovery, providing a prior for the denoising module in message passing. Score-based generative models assume the input signal to be a clean signal perturbed by independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) Gaussian noise. Yet, this assumption often fails during message passing, leading to slow convergence and limited reconstruction quality. In this letter, we propose the learned score-based turbo message passing (LSTMP) algorithm to overcome this limitation. By employing a deep unfolding framework, LSTMP unrolls the iterative process into a neural network, enabling the denoiser to adapt to complicated noise distributions inherent in the iterative updates. Furthermore, by treating the denoiser as an iterative solver, we incorporate the likelihood information of compressed measurements into the neural network of the denoiser, resembling the message passing between linear and nonlinear modules in STMP. Experiments on the FFHQ dataset show that LSTMP achieves convergence in no more than three iterations while delivering high-ffdelity re-constructions, signiffcantly outperforming the existing baselines.

</details>


### [371] [From Tokens to Pixels: Training-Free Guided Upsampling of Frozen CARL Features for Hyperspectral Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27541)
*Dominik Fromme,Ellen Markowski,Tim Streckert,Jörg Thiem*

Abstract: Camera-agnostic foundation models such as CARL map spectral images from arbitrary sensors into a shared feature space, but deliver their features only on a token grid at one eighth of the input resolution. The usual bilinear interpolation blurs material boundaries over about one token and rounds off corners. We investigate how much spatial accuracy image-guided upsampling recovers for frozen CARL features without training the encoder or the upsampler. We compare joint bilateral upsampling (JBU), a guided filter and the pretrained AnyUp, each with the hyperspectral image or an RGB rendering as the guide. A hyperspectral ColorChecker dataset acquired specifically for this study, with model-independent reference geometry measured to sub-pixel precision, serves as the evaluation benchmark. JBU with the hyperspectral guide raises the Boundary IoU from 13 to 67 % and reduces the effective edge width of the features from 38 to 4 px, close to the resolution of the raw spectra. The effect occurs in all 24 patches of the ColorChecker and replicates on an additional recording. On placenta and oral cavity images, the feature edges also become markedly qualitatively sharper. For sharp, high-contrast boundaries, JBU is the most accurate and most economical method. On tissue with softer transitions, the pretrained AnyUp yields qualitatively sharper contours. An analysis of 78 simulated band configurations shows that the gain persists in every configuration as long as the head matches the configuration. Image-guided upsampling is thus a simple, training-free step for applications that use CARL frozen with linear heads.

</details>

Abstract: Ultrasound time-harmonic elastography (THE) is a non-invasive imaging technique that measures soft-tissue stiffness. Relying on the same principle of time-harmonic shear wave excitation as established magnetic resonance elastography (MRE), THE offers full field-of-view stiffness mapping at short acquisition times and sub-second temporal resolution across organs. However, reconstruction of shear wave speed maps from acquired tissue wavefields remains challenging due to speckle noise and sparse spatial coverage of the lateral component.
  Deep learning methods have shown promising results for image reconstruction in transient-based ultrasound elastography modalities but remain unexplored in THE. In MRE, MF-ElastoNet, a 2D multifrequency neural network-based wave reconstruction method trained on synthetic physics-based wave data, has shown high generalizability across dynamic ranges and resolutions.
  We investigated the potential of a multifrequency neural network-based wave inversion for THE, by performing a first feasibility analysis. To this end, we adapted the processing pipeline of MF-ElastoNet from MRE to THE. We tested our approach on THE acquired phantom data from the algorithms for mapping elastic modulus Challenge, as well as on in vivo liver THE in 4 healthy volunteers and 14 patients with focal liver lesions, including benign focal nodular hyperplasia, and malignant hepatocellular carcinoma and adenocarcinoma. MF-ElastoNet was successfully adapted to THE with in vivo liver testing. Compared with k-MDEV, the state-of-the-art wave inversion method for THE, Bland-Altman analysis showed a mean bias of -0.02 m/s (95% limits of agreement: -0.36 to 0.33 m/s).

</details>

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Underwater imaging suffers from wavelength-dependent absorption and scattering that distort scenes and obscure spectral signatures. While RGB restoration improves visual clarity, the outputs remain broadband and tied to the sensor's color space. We introduce a physically grounded framework that lifts underwater restoration to continuous spectral radiance using multi-view hyperspectral imaging. By representing continuous spectra via low-rank basis functions and leveraging multi-distance observations, our framework jointly constrains scene radiance and water parameters. We employ a robust sparse-to-dense pipeline: water parameters are estimated from consistent multi-view matches and then applied globally to invert the light transport model across the full images. Validated on physics-based synthetic data rendered via the Mitsuba 3 spectral path tracer, our method recovers physically meaningful radiance spectra and, as a downstream application, outperforms standard image restoration baselines.

</details>
